## Page 3


Preface
This brief, in the form of an e-book, is a collection of notes that cover elementary and medium level
number theory with a target audience of primarily computer science students. It can be used in the
number theory portion of a discrete mathematics course, or a course on the mathematical foundations
of computer science, or as background material for a cryptography course.
Thematically it is split into five areas that map to chapters. The first chapter is introductory and covers
topics including divisibility, prime numbers, and modular arithmetic including modular linear equations.
The second chapter covers additional topics such as Euler’s totient function, units and inverses, the
Chinese remainder theorem, and Fermat’s and Euler’s theorems. The following chapter covers primitive
roots, quadratic residues, the Jacobi and Legendre symbols, Gauss’s lemma and Eisenstein’s theorem,
and briefly discusses applications of number theory to cryptography.
The fourth chapter is focused
on traditional primality testing methods covering Miller’s algorithms, Rabin’s conversion of a Miller
algorithm into a probabilistic primality test algorithm, Solovay-Strassen’s algorithm and several other
peripheral results including Carmichael numbers and the equivalence of Miller’s two algorithms. Finally
the last brief chapter can be viewed as an introduction to more advanced elements of number theory and
its coverage includes multiplicative functions, the M¨obius function, Dirichlet products and Dirichlet and
M¨obius inversions.
Different parts of this e-book are for freshman to senior undergraduate students in computing and
in particular computer science. Graduate students with limited exposure to number theory can use it to
acquire a background suitable for typical cryptography courses at the master’s level.

## Page 9


Chapter 1
Introductory Number Theory
1.1
Numbers
Definition 1.1
Integers
The set of integers is denoted as Z.
Z = {...,−3,−2,−1,0,1,2,3,...}.
Definition 1.2
Natural integer numbers
A natural integer number, or natural number, or ordinal number is a non-negative integer number.
The set of natural numbers is denoted as N.
N = {0,1,2,3,...}.
This definition varies in different textbooks. Although we define a natural integer number as a non-
negative integer number, several alternative sources describe a natural integer number as a positive
integer number thus excluding zero.
Definition 1.3
Positive and negative integers
The set of positive integers is denoted as Z+, the set of negative integers is denoted as Z−, and the
set of non-zero integers is denoted as Z∗.
Z+ = {1,2,3,...}.
Z−= {−1,−2,−3,...}.
Z∗= {±1,±2,±3,...}.
For the positive and negative integers one may also use Z∗
+ and Z∗
−. Z is the set of integers (positive,
negative or zero). For a positive integer n > 0 the set Zn sometimes denoted as Z/nZ or Z/n is the set
of integers modulo n, thus representing the n equivalence classes that the integers of Z can be split into
depending on the remainder of their division by n.
Integer one is an integer, a rational and algebraic and thus a real number. Number 4/5 is rational and
algebraic and thus a real number. Number
√
2 is irrational, algebraic and thus a real number. Number
π is irrational, transcendental and thus a real number.

## Page 10

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
1.2
Divisibility and compositeness
Definition 1.4
Divisibility
The symbol for divide is |. Let a ∈Z,b ∈Z,a ̸= 0. We write a|b, a ̸= 0, and read a divides b, if there
exists an integer q ∈Z such that b = aq.
Integer a is then a divisor or factor of b and b is a multiple of a. When a divides b we can also say b is
divided (evenly) by a or b is divisible by a, or equivalently the division of b by a leaves a remainder of
zero. For a|b, a is never a zero; b can be zero.
For a division of b divided by a, integer b is the dividend in such a division. Integer a is the divisor
of b also known as a factor of b. The quotient q is q = b/a = ⌊b/a⌋, and the integer remainder r is
r = b−a∗q. The remainder is unique if 0 ≤r < |b|. If a|b then q = b/a is an integer and r = 0.
We say a does not divide b if there is no such q ∈Z such that b = aq. We then write a ∤b.
Definition 1.5
If a does not divide b we write a ∤b instead.
Example 1.2.1
The following integers divide 50.
1,2,5,10,25,50.
We usually write down the positive divisors. The negative divisors are implied. Therefore a complete
list of divisors would be as follows.
±1,±2,±5,±10,±25,±50.
Definition 1.6
Trivial divisor
The trivial divisors of n are 1 and n. If one includes negative numbers, they are 1, −1, n and −n.
Definition 1.7
Odd, even
An integer n is even if it is a multiple of two. Otherwise it is an odd integer.
Example 1.2.2
Every integer a is a divisor of 0 that is, ∀a ∈Z we have a|0. 0 is a divisor of itself and only itself.
Solution. Since 0 = a·0 the first claim follows: a is a divisor of 0. Since 0 = 0·q, integer 0 is a divisor
of 0. There is no way for any d ̸= 0 to have d = 0·q. Thus 0 cannot be the divisor of any d ̸= 0. Thus
0 only divides 0; moreover, 0 is a multiple of every integer!
Example 1.2.3
Both 5 and −5 are divisors of 5. Both 1 and −1 are divisors of 5.
Solution. It is 5 = 5 ·1 and 5 = (−5)(−1). Replacing 5 by a, for every integer a, both ±a and ±1 are
divisors of a. Thus integer 5 has four divisors +1,−1,+5,−5. And so so does −5. And in fact any
b ̸= 0,1,−1 has at least four divisors which are ±b,±1. 0 has an infinite number of divisors (in facts its
set of divisors is Z). Only +1,−1 have two divisors each (including the other one of the pair). +1 is the
positive unit and −1 the negative unit and collectively are known as the units of Z.


## Page 11


1.2. DIVISIBILITY AND COMPOSITENESS
3
Example 1.2.4
If a|b then −a|b,
a|−b,
a|−b.
Solution. If b = aq then b = (−a)(−q) and −b = a(−q) and −b = a(−q).
Example 1.2.5
If a|b and a|c, then a|b±c.
Solution. We have from a|b that b = am for some m ∈Z. Likewise c = an for some n ∈Z. Adding or
subtracting we have b±c = a(m±n) and the result follows.
Example 1.2.6
If a|b and b ̸= 0 then |a| ≤|b|.
Solution. Since a|b we have that b = am for some m ∈Z. Since b ̸= 0 we have a ̸= 0 as well. Furthermore
m ̸= 0 as well. Thus |m| ≥1. And thus |b| = |am| = |a||m| ≥|a|.
Theorem 1.1
Properties of divisibility
If a,b,c,d,k,m ∈Z, then
(a) 1|a. a|0 and 0|0. Moreover, 0|a implies a = 0.
(b1) a|a and of course ±a|±a.
(b2) a|b∧b|c ⇒a|c.
(c1) If a|b then for every k ∈Z, a|kb.
(c2) If a|b then for every k ∈Z, ka|kb.
(d1) if a|b and a|c then a|b+c.
(d2)
if a|b and a|c then a|b−c.
(d3)
if a|b and a|c then a|kb±mc.
(f1)
if a|b and b ̸= 0 then |a| ≤|b|.
(f2)
if a|b and a,b > 0 then a ≤b.
(f3)
if a|b and b|a then |a| = |b|.
(g)
if ka|kb and k ̸= 0 then a|b.
Proof.
(a) a = 1 · a implies 1|a. 0 = a·0 implies a|0. 0 = 0·0 implies 0|0, and a = 0·q implies a = 0 and this
concludes the case.
(b1) a = a·1 implies a|a. Moreover −a = a·(−1) concludes the case.
(b2) If a|b, then b = aq, for some q ∈Z. If b|c, then c = br, for some r ∈Z. This implies c = b · r =
(a·q)·r = a·(qr) i.e. a|c.
(c1) If a|b, then b = aq, for some q ∈Z. Then kb = kaq = a(kq) i.e. a|kb.
(c2) If a|b, then b = aq, for some q ∈Z. Then kb = kaq = (ka)q i.e. ka|kb.

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
(d1) If a|b, then b = aq, for some q ∈Z. If a|c, then c = ar, for some r ∈Z. Then b+c = a(q+r) implies
a|b+c.
(d2) Moreover for the a,b,c of (d1) we have b−c = a(q−r) implies a|b−c.
(d3) Furthermore for the a,b,c of (d1) we have kb±mc = a(kq±mr) implies a|kb±mc.
(f1) If a|b then b = aq. Then |b| = |aq| = |a| · |q|. If b ̸= 0 then |b| > 0. (Absolute values are positive
or zero.) This implies that |a| > 0 and |q| > 0. The latter is equivalent to |q| ≥1. Then |b| = |a|·|q| ≥
|a|·1 ≥|a|. Equivalently |a| ≤|b|.
(f2) If all of a,b are positive we can drop the absolute values from (f1) concluding a ≤b.
(f3) From (f1) we have |a| ≤|b|. If b|a we can likewise conclude that |b| ≤|a| thus deriving |a| = |b|.
(g) if ka|kb and k ̸= 0 then kb = (ka)q. For non-zero k dividing both sides we have b = a · q and thus
a|b.
Theorem 1.2
Uniqueness
For every a ∈Z∗,b ∈Z, if a|b, there is a unique integer q ∈Z such that b = aq.
Proof. Suppose that a|b i.e. b = aq with q ∈Z and a ∈Z∗i.e. a ̸= 0. If q is not unique, then there
might exist a q1 such that b = aq1 and q ̸= q1. Then b = aq = aq1 implies a(q−q1) = 0. Since a ̸= 0, it
must be q−q1 = 0. Then q = q1 but this contradicts to the existence of q1 ̸= q.


## Page 13


1.3. PRIMES
5
1.3
Primes
Definition 1.8
Prime numbers
A natural number p > 1 is a prime number if it is not the product of two smaller natural numbers.
For example for natural number 5 the only product that gives 5 is 5·1. In that product 5 is not a smaller
natural number than 5 and thus 5 is prime. Natural number 6 is not a prime number: 6 = 2·3 and both
2,3 are smaller (natural numbers) than 6.
Definition 1.9
Prime
An integer p ∈Z∗
+ is a prime (number) if and only if p ̸= 1 and its only positive divisors are 1 and p.
An integer p ∈Z∗is a prime (number) if and only if p ̸= ±1 and its only divisors are the units +1
and −1 of Z and p, and −p
A number that is not prime and not a unit (+1 or −1) is a composite number.
Definition 1.10
Composite
An integer n ∈Z∗such that n ̸= ±1, it is either a prime or a composite (integer) number.
Definition 1.11
1 is a unit
1 is neither a prime number nor a composite number. It is a unit.
Lemma 1.1
Composite
An integer n ∈Z+∗is composite if and only if it has a factor a such that 1 < a < n. Then there is
another factor q such that 1 < q < n, and q = n/a.
Proof. If n is composite, then n is a multiple of integer a that is neither 1 nor n. Then there exists q
such that n = aq for some integer q. If a is neither 1 nor n then 1 < a < n. (We also used (f2) from
Theorem 1.1.) Since n = aq, q is also positive and q > 1. Since a > 1, we have n = aq > q implies q < n.
And since a is not n then q cannot be 1.
Theorem 1.3
Composite with a prime factor
An integer n ∈Z∗with n > 1 has a prime factor p such that p|n.
Proof. Let Q be the set of natural integer numbers greater than one, that have no prime factors. We
shall show that Q = /0.
Say Q is not empty. Then there is a minimum element say m ∈Q. Since m|m, and m has no prime
factors, m cannot be a prime number. Thus m is composite and let m = qr, where 1 < q,r < m. Since
either q or r is < m, and m is the smallest element of Q it means q ̸∈Q. By definition q has a prime
factor p i.e. p|q and q|m. By transitivity p|m. The latter contradicts the fact that m being in Q it should
not have prime factors (such as p). Thus Q has no minimum element m and thus it is empty!
Theorem 1.4
A factor less than √n for n
For a composite integer n > 1 one of its prime factors(divisors) is less than or equal to √n.
Proof. Let a,b be such that n = ab. We have 1 < a ≤b < n. If a > √n then b ≥a > √n and therefore
n = ab > √n·√n = n, which is impossible. It follows that 1 < a ≤√n. Thus a has a prime factor p by
Lemma 1.3 and p ≤a. Since p|a and a|n we conclude that p|n, with p ≤a ≤√n.

Example 1.3.1
Show that if 2|ab for a,b ∈Z, then 2|a or 2|b.
Solution. If neither 2|a nor 2|b, then a,b are odd numbers and thus a = 2n + 1 and b = 2m + 1. Then
since ab is an even number by way of 2|ab we obtain
ab = (2n+1)(2m+1) = 4mn+2m+2n+1 = 2(2mn+m+n)+1,
which is clearly an odd integer number and thus not even contradicting the fact that ab is even!
Example 1.3.2
Prime numbers are irreducible. Thus if p is a prime number then p cannot be written in the form
p = qr where q,r are both non units. (In other words for an irreducible number either q or r is a unit.)
Solution. Note 0 cannot be a prime number therefore p ̸= 0. Say prime number p is not irreducible. If
a prime number p is not irreducible, then p = qr where neither q nor r is a unit. Then p|qr. Moreover
q|p and r|p, and therefore |q| ≤|p| and |r| ≤|p|. Then |q| ≤|p| and |r| ≤|p|. We claim p ∤q and p ∤r.
If this was not the case (say the former) then p|q i.e. |p| ≤|q| which with |q| ≤|p| implies q = ±p and
then r = ±1. This contradicts the assumption that r is not a unit.
1.3.1
Inifinitely many primes
Lemma 1.2
A theorem by Euclid
There are infinitely many prime numbers.
Proof. If there are only finite prime numbers and let them ALL be p1,..., pm, where p1 = 2. Then form
integer n = p1 · p2 ·...· pm +1. There are two possibilities for n: (a) it is a prime number, (b) it is not a
prime number.
Case 1. If n is a prime number Then n = 2 · ... · pi · ... · pm + 1 >> 2pi + 1 > pi given that all pi > 1.
We have just found one more prime number beyond the m ones that were declared ALL that there are:
a contradiction.
Case 2. If n is a composite number, let p be a prime factor of n, i.e. p|n with p > 1. Such a p exists by
the previous Lemma (composite with a prime factor). This p cannot be one of the m p1,..., pm. Why
? if p was say p = pi then p|pi implies by Theorem 1.1 (c1,d3) that p|p1 · p2 · ... · pm. Since p|n and
n = p1 · p2 ·...· pm +1 the p divides their difference i.e. p|1 by Theorem 1.1 (d2). This means p is one
by Theorem 1.1 (f2). But one is a unit not a prime number (plus also the fact that p > 1).

1.4. INTEGER DIVISION

1.4
Integer division
Theorem 1.5
Division
For a ∈Z and b ∈Z∗there exist unique integers q ∈Z and r ∈Z such that
a = bq+r
,
0 ≤r < |b|.
Proof.
Case 1 (a ≥0,b > 0). Let A be the set of all non-negative integers a−bi, where i is such that a−bi ≥0.
For i = 0, a belongs to A, and thus A is not empty. By the Well-ordered set principle A has a minimum
and let it be r. Since r is in A, we have r ≥0 and r = a−bi for some integer i. All it remains to show is
that r < |b| = b. Say that r ≥b instead. Then r −b ≥0. Moreover
r −b = (a−bi)−b = a−b(i+1).
Thus r −b ≥0 and is of the form a −bi′, with i′ = i + 1. Thus it belongs to A. Moreover r −b is less
than r, r −b < r since b is positive. We have found an element smaller than the minimum element of A.
This contradicts to the choice of r as being the minimum; we reached contradiction because we assume
r ≥b. Thus r < b. This thus establishes that 0 ≤r < b.
We have yet to prove the pair (q,r) is unique. Let is not be and let another pair be (q′,r′). Then
a = bq+r = bq′ +r′, where 0 ≤r,r′ < b. This gives b(q−q′) = r′ −r, and |b(q−q′)| = |r′ −r|. Adding
0 ≤r and r′ < b we get r′ < r + b or equivalently r′ −r < b. Thus |b(q −q′)| < b. As b > 0 we have
|q−q′| < 1. This can only be possible, for q = q′ since q,q′ are integer. Then from a = bq+r = bq′ +r′
if q = q′, we deduce also that r = r′. This answers positively the question about the uniqueness of the
pair (q,r) for case 1.
Case 2 (a < 0,b > 0). Similarly as before A is not empty because a−bi ≥0 contains at least one element
a−ba ≥0, and the rest of the discussion is similar to case 1.
Case 3 (a ∈Z,b < 0). Then |b| > 0, and of course |b| = −b. By way of cases 1 and 2 for a and |b| we
have that a = |b|q+r, where 0 ≤r < |b|. This is equivalent to a = (−b)q+r, where 0 ≤r < |b|. This is
equivalent to a = b(−q)+r, where 0 ≤r < |b|. The claim is satisfied for case 3 as well.
Conclusion. For a pair (a,b ̸= 0) there is a unique pair (q,r) with a = bq+r and 0 ≤r < |b|.
Example 1.4.1
Find the quotient and the remainder of the division of 37 by 5.
Then find the quotient and the
remainder of the division of -37 by 5.
Solution.
(a) For 37 we have the following
37 = 5·7+2.
37 is the divident, 5 is the divisor and 7 the quotient. The remainder is 2. Note that 0 ≤2 < |5|.
(b) For −37 we have the following
−37 = 5·(−8)+3.
−37 is the divident, 5 is the divisor and −8 the quotient. The remainder is 3. Note that 0 ≤3 < |5|.
One can also observe that
−37 = 5·(−7)−2.
However only the remainder 3 is one that satisfies the 0 ≤3 < |5| requirement and is unique.

Division results

Theorem 1.7
Remainder of division by b
Let a,A ∈Z with b|a and b|A. The remainders of the divisions of a,A with b are the same if and only
if a−A is a multiple of b.
Proof. ⇒. If a = bq+r and A = bQ+R, and we are given r = R, the a−A = bq+r−qQ−r = b(q−Q).
Since q−Q ∈Z, we conclude that b|a−A.

Then let a = bq+r and A = bQ+R, where 0 ≤r,R < |b|. From the former a−A = bm and A = bQ+R
we have a−(bQ+R) = bm implying a = b(m+Q)+R. This latter equality implies q = m+Q and r = R
as R is such that 0 ≤R < |b|). Result is proven.

Lemma 1.3
Integer d such that d|n if and only if the remainder of the division of n by d is 0.
Proof.
⇒(only-if).
If d is such that d|n this means n = qd that is n = qd +0. Given the uniqueness of q,r from Theorem 1.5
we conclude q = q and r = 0.
⇐(if).

1.5
Greatest common divisor
Definition 1.12
Greatest common divisor
For integers a,b ∈Z, gcd(a,b) is the greatest common divisor of a and b if and only if gcd(a,b)|a
and gcd(a,b)|b and every other divisor c of a,b is such that c ≤gcd(a,b). Therefore,
(i) gcd(a,b)|a and gcd(a,b)|b,
(ii) c|a and c|b ⇒c ≤gcd(a,b).
Moreover, gcd(a,b) ≤|a| and gcd(a,b) ≤|b|.
Note that if c|a then by Theorem 1.1(v) we have |c| ≤|a|. Likewise if c|b we have |c| ≤|b|. Combining
the two we have |c| ≤max(|a|,|b|). Thus the set of common divisors of a,b is finite. A finite set of
integers always has a maximum, and thus there is a uniquest largest integer d > 0 such that d|a and d|b.
We call d the greatest common divisor of a,b and denote it by gcd(a,b) thus d = gcd(a,b).
Example 1.5.1
(a) The greatest common divisor of 1 and n is 1.
(b) If a|b then gcd(a,b) = b.
(c) gcd(5,15) = 5. gcd(30,105) = 15.
(d) The common divisors of 30 and 105 are {1,3,5,15,}. If we include negative numbers then it is
{±1,±3,±5,±15,}, twice as many.
Note 1.1
The gcd(0,0) is not defined as the set of common divisors is an infinite set and it does not have a
maximum. In the remainder when gcd(a,b) is considered, we would assume that a ̸= 0 or b ̸= 0 (or
both). Thus it cannot be that both a and b are zero. One way to express this is by having |a|+|b| ̸= 0.

Definition 1.13
Let S(a) be the set of divisors of a.
Fact 1.1
Simple GCD facts
Let a,b ∈Z such that |a|+|b| ̸= 0. Then the following apply.
(i)
gcd(a,b) > 0 and also gcd(a,b) ≥1.
(ii)
gcd(a,b) = gcd(|a|,|b|).
(iii)
gcd(a,b) = gcd(b,a).
(iv)
gcd(a,1) = 1.
(v)
gcd(a,0) = |a| for all a ̸= 0.
(vi)
gcd(a,b) = |a| if and only if a|b.
Proof.
(i) Obviously. gcd(a,b) is the maximum of the common divisors of a and b i.e. the maximum elements
of S(a)∩S(b). One positive element of this set of common divisors is 1 and thus gcd(a,b) ≥1 in addition
to gcd(a,b) > 0.
(ii) Since S(a) = S(|a|) and S(b) = S(|b|) we have that S(a) ∩S(b) = S(|a|) ∩S(|b|). Thus gcd(a,b) is
equal to gcd(|a|,|b|) since the set of common divisors are equal to each other.
(iii) It is a consequence of the fact that S(a)∩S(b) = S(b)∩S(a).
(iv) Since 1|a trivially, and the largest divisor of 1 is 1 itself the result follows.
(Note that S(1) =
{−1,+1}.)
(v) S(a)∩S(0) = S(a)∩Z = S(a). The result follows as the largest element of S(a) is |a|.
(vi)
⇒. If gcd(a,b) = |a| then |a||a and |a||b. For the latter there exist q such that b = q|a|. If a is non-
negative, the b = qa as well. If a is negative b = q(−a) = (−q)a. The former concludes a|b and so does
the latter.
⇐. If a|b then −a|b and thus |a||b. Since trivially a|a we conclude that −a|a and thus |a||a. Then
|a||gcd(a,b). This by Theorem 1.1(v) implies |a| ≤gcd(a,b) (the gcd is always positive thus no absolute
value sign around it is needed). By definition gcd(a,b)|a and thus gcd(a,b)|−a. Thus |gcd(a,b)| ≤|a|.
Combining |gcd(a,b)| ≤|a| and |a| ≤gcd(a,b) the result follows.

Theorem 1.9
If x,y ∈Z, then
gcd(y,x) = gcd(x,y) = gcd(x,xq+y)
for all integers q ∈Z.
Proof. Let d = gcd(x,y). Let d1 = gcd(x,xq+y). If d|x and d|y by divisilibity we have x = dx1 and thus
xq = dx1q and then we have d|xq and d|y. Thus d|xq+y in addition to d|x. Thus d is a common divisor
of xq+y and x. Thus d ≤d1 = gcd(x,xq+y). For d1 = gcd(x,xq+y) we have d1|x and d1|xq+y. From
the former, we conclude that d1|xq; combining it with the latter we have d1|xq+y−xq i.e. d1|y. Thus
d1|x and d1|y and therefore d1 is a common divisor of x,y. Thus d1 ≤d = gcd(x,y). By way of d1 ≤d
and d ≤d1 we conclude d = d1.
From Theorem 1.5 in order to compute the gcd(a,b) we formulate the division operation.
a = bq+r
where 0 ≤r < |a|. Then by way of Theorem 1.9
gcd(a,b) = gcd(b,a) = gcd(b,bq+r) Th. 1.9
=
gcd(b,r).
For calculating the gcd(a,b) at a minimum |a| + |b| ̸= 0 that is, we can’t determine the gcd of two
numbers that are both 0. The set of common divisors is then Z and has no maximum. If b = 0 then
gcd(a,b) = a. If a = b then gcd(a,b) = a. Thus in general we need to compute gcd(a,b) for a > b or
b > a. If b < a then we rename (swap) a and b so that they become a > b. We may assume without loss
of generality that a > b.
1.5.1
The gcd algorithm by Euclid
Theorem 1.10
GCD: Euclid’s algorithm
For a > b, b ̸= 0 the gcd(a,b) can be calculated using iterated division, as follows.
a = bq+r
0 ≤r < |b|
gcd(a,b) = gcd(b,r)
b = rq1 +r1
0 ≤r1 < r
gcd(b,r) = gcd(r,r1)
r = r1q2 +r2
0 ≤r2 < r1
gcd(r,r1) = gcd(r1,r2)
...
rk−2 = rk−1qk +rk
0 ≤rk < rk−1
gcd(rk−2,rk−1) = gcd(rk−1,rk)
rk−1 = rkqk+1 +0
gcd(rk−1,rk) = gcd(rk,0) = rk
The last non-zero remainder is the gcd:
gcd(a,b) = rk.
Moreover, for a > b the sequence of steps is finite as the sequence of remainders is a decreasing
sequence of positive numbers eventually reaching zero since |b| > r > r1 > r2 > ... > rk−1 > rk > 0.
Proof.
Finite number of steps.
The sequence of remainders is a decreasing sequence starting below |b|. Thus after no more that |b|
steps it will reach a remainder of 0.


## Page 20


12
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
Special and trivial cases.
If the numbers are negative we consider their absolute values and continue. If a = b = 0, there is no
gcd(a,b) as S(0) = Z. If a > b = 0, then gcd(a,b) = a. If b > a = 0, then gcd(a,b) = b. We deal with
some more trivial cases next. Otherwise, if a = b then gcd(a,b) = a = b.
Bottom-to-top proof.
Starting from the bottom rk|rk−1. Then from the penultimate equation we have rk dividing both itself
and rk−1 and thus rk|rk−1qk + rk. Thus rk|rk−2. Working likewise we show that rk divides r and b of
the first equation and thus also divides a. Thus rk divides both a,b. Therefore rk ≤gcd(a,b). Pick an
arbitrary integer d dividing a and b. Working downwards we show that d divides rk as well. Thus d ≤rk.
Set d = gcd(a,b). This translates into gcd(a,b) ≤rk; combined with rk ≤gcd(a,b) leads to rk = gcd(a,b),
as needed.
Top-to-bottom proof: start with a > b > 0.
If b > a > 0, we swap a and b. Thus for all remaining cases we assume that a > b > 0 in the remainder.
Therefore, the algorithm computes gcd(a,b) for a > b and b ̸= 0. Furthermore, |a|+|b| ̸= 0. It is obvious
from the statement that by using Theorem 1.9 the following apply.
gcd(a,b) = gcd(b,r) = gcd(r,r1) = gcd(r1,r2) = ... = gcd(ri−1,ri) = ... = gcd(rk−1,rk) = gcd(rk,0) = rk.
That is, the last, non-zero remainder, is the gcd of a,b. Moreover the sequence of remainders purely
decreases i.e. |b| > r > r1 > r2 > ... > rk−1 > rk. Thus after a finite number of no more than b steps rk
will be determined.
We use Theorem 1.5 repeteadly starting with x = b and y = a.
gcd(x,y) = gcd(y,x) ⇒gcd(a,b) = gcd(b,a) ⇒gcd(a,b) = gcd(b,bq+r),
and continuing with x = b and y = r and proceeding similarly.
gcd(x,y) = gcd(x,xq+y) ⇒gcd(b,r) = gcd(b,bq+r) ⇒
It is obvious from the statement of the theorem and the previous derivations that
gcd(a,b) = gcd(b,r) = gcd(r,r1) = gcd(r1,r2) = ... = gcd(ri−1,ri) = ... = gcd(rk−1,rk) = gcd(rk,0) = rk
That is, the last, non-zero remainder, is the gcd. Moreover the sequence of remainders purely decreases
|b| > r > r1 > r2 > ... > rk−1 > rk. Thus after a finite number of no more than |b| steps rk will be
determined.
For the golden ration φ we have that φ ≈1.6183, and lgφ ≈0.6942 and 1/lgφ ≈1.44.
Lemma 1.4
(a) In Euclid’s algorithm the maximum number of division steps k + 2 is for a,b that are Fibonacci
numbers.
(b) For a,b ∈Z, with a > b > 0 the number of divisions in Euclid’s Algorithm is at most lgb/lgφ +2 ≈
1.44lgb+2, where φ = (1+
√
5)/2 is the golden ratio.
Proof.
The worst-case number of division steps is for two consecutive Fibonacci numbers, say a = Fn and
b = Fn−1, where F0 = 0 and F1 = 1. Then the first division is, Fn = Fn−1 + Fn−2, n > 1, and the last
division F2 = F1 + F0, where F0 = 0 and F1 = 1. All quotients generated are one, the smallest possible
quotient. The total number of divisions is n −1. A bound would be provided for n−1 with respect to
b.
Note that Fn ≥φ n−2 for all n ≥2. This follows from φ2 = φ +1 and induction on n. Clearly F2 = 1 ≥φ 0.
Moreover
Fn = Fn−1 +Fn−2 ≥φ n−3 +φ n−4 ≥(1+φ)φn−4 ≥φ 2φ n−4 ≥φ n−2.

1.6. EXTENDED GCD
13
If the first division involves a > b, this means
b ≥Fn−1 ≥φ n−3 ⇔n−1 ≤lgb/lgφ +2.
Then the number of division steps n −1 is upper bounded by 1.44lgb + 2. Of course the last division
step is trivial, so the bound is in fact at most 1.44lgb+1.
The worst-case (i.e. longest) division is for two consecutive Fibonacci numbers Fn = Fn−1 + Fn−2,
n > 1, with F0 = 0 and F1 = 1 generate a quotient of 1 every iteration! For Fn the number of divisions is
n−2 as the last division is F2 = F1 +F0 = F1 +0. The n-th Fibonacci number exceeds φ n−2.
Corollary 1.1
For a,b < N the number of divisions in Euclid’s Algorithm will be less than lgN/lgφ ≈1.48lgN.
Proof. We can do a bit better by bounding the number of divisions in terms of b only. In the worst case,
for an n step division a ≥Fn+2 and b ≥Fn+1. But Fn+1 ≥φ n−1. Thus n−1 ≤lgb/lgφ. lgφ ≈0.687 and
thus n−1 ≤1.471lgb i.e. n ≤1.471lgN +1.
1.6
Extended GCD
Theorem 1.11
Extended-GCD
B´ezout identity. Let a,b ∈Z, where |a|+|b| ̸= 0. Let d = gcd(a,b) then there exist integers x,y such
that
d = ax+by
(1.6)
Moreover, d is the smallest positive integer that can be written as a linear combination of a,b.
Proof. Consider
A = {au+bv|u,v ∈Z ∧au+bv > 0}.
Note that for u = a, v = b, a·a+b·b > 0 since |a|+|b| ̸= 0. Set A has thus at least one element and it
is not empty. Thus from the well-ordered set principle there must exist a minimum element for A and let
it be d′. Since d′ ∈A there exist x,y such that
ax+by = d′.
A. Show d′ is a divisor of a. We first show that d′|a. Let us form the division operation for the two
integers i.e.
a = d′q′ +r′
where 0 ≤r′ < d′. Then we have that
r′ = a−d′q′ = a−(ax+by)q′ = a·(1−xq′)+b·(−yq′).
Note that because of the division of a by d′ we know that 0 ≤rprime. We examine two cases.
Case 1: 0 < r′.
If r′ > 0 then it is a member of A since r′ = a·(1−xq′)+b·(−yq′). But this cannot happen since r′ < d′
and d′ is the minimum element of A. It would imply the existence of an element of A (i.e. r′) smaller
than the minimum element of A (i.e. d′)!
Case 2: 0 = r′. The only other possibility is that r′ = 0. But then, a = d′q′ +r′ implies a = d′q′ i.e. d′ is
a divisor of a.

14
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
B. Show d′ is a divisor of b. Similar to the case involving a.
Conclusion.
Thus d′ divides a and b and is a common divisor of a,b. It should be that d′ ≤d since d is the gcd of a
and b and is the greatest common divisor of a,b. Furthermore, d|a and d|b and therefore d|ax+by and
thus d|d′. This implies d ≤d′ This d ≤d′ along with the d′ ≤d shows that d = d′. In conclusion
d = gcd(a,b) = d′ = ax+by.
1.6.1
Some corollaries
Corollary 1.2
Let a,b ∈Z, where |a|+|b| ̸= 0. Let d = gcd(a,b). Show that set A defined as follows
A = {au+bv|u,v ∈Z ∧au+bv > 0},
contains multiples of d.
Proof. By Theorem 1.11, the smallest positive element of A was proven to be d. Moreover every element
z of A is of the form z = au + bv. Since d|a and d|b we have that d|au + bv that is d|z. Thus every
element of A is a multiple of d.
Corollary 1.3
Let a,b ∈Z, where |a| + |b| ̸= 0. Let a,b be such that gcd(a,b) = 1. Then there exist x,y ∈Z such
that
ax+by = 1.
Proof. From Theorem 1.11 and Corollary 1.2 we know that there exist x,y such that ax+by = gcd(a,b).
Since a,b are such that gcd(a,b) = 1, we obtain ax+by = 1, as needed.
Corollary 1.4
Let a|bc and gcd(a,b) = 1. Then a|c.
Proof. From the previous problem we have that there exist x,y such that ax+by = 1. Then,
acx+bcy = c.
Since we are given a|bc we conclude a|bcy. Moreover, a|acx. Then a|acx+bcy = a|c, as needed.
Corollary 1.5
If d = gcd(a,b) then S(a)∩S(b) = S(d). (In other words, m|a and m|b ⇐⇒m|d.)
Proof. Every common divisor of a and b is a divisor of d = gcd(a,b) since d = d′ = ax+by derived and
used in Theorem 1.11. That is S(a)∩S(b) ⊆S(d).
Moreover for t ∈S(d) then t|d and since d|a and d|b by transitivity we have t|a and t|b.
The
former show that t ∈S(a) and the latter that t ∈S(b). Both of them show that t ∈S(a) ∩S(b). Thus
S(d) ⊆S(a)∩S(b).

gcd(ma,mb) = mgcd(a,b).

Corollary 1.7
For three a,b,c ∈Z, we have gcd(a,b,c) = gcd((a,b),c) = gcd(d,c), where d = gcd(a,b).
Proof. gcd(a,b,c) belongs to S(a)∩S(b)∩S(c) = (S(a)∩S(b))∩S(c). The result follows.

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
Corollary 1.8
For p a prime, and a ∈Z∗, p ∤a if and only if gcd(a, p) = 1.
Proof. If p is a prime S(p) = {−1,+1,−p,+p}, then p does not divide a means that neither p nor −p
are divisors of a. The only possible divisors are +1 and -1. Thus S(a)∩S(p) = {+1,−1}. Thus the gcd
is 1, and thus gcd(a, p) = 1 as needed.
If gcd(a, p) = 1, then p cannot divide a. This is because if p|a since obviously p|p we would have
from a prior property that p|gcd(a, p) and (in fact gcd(a, p) = |p|). For this to happen p should be −1
or +1. But p is prime and this can’t happen.
Corollary 1.9
For a,b,m ∈Z∗and gcd(a,b) = 1 and a|bm then a|m.
Proof. Since gcd(a,b) = 1 we have 1 = ax+by for some x,y. Multiplying by m we get m = axm+bmy.
Obviously a|axm. Since a|m we have a|bmy as well. Thus a|axm+bmy i.e. a|m.
Corollary 1.10
For a,b ∈Z∗and prime p is such that p|ab, then p divides either a or b.
Proof. Say p does not divide a. By a prior theorem since p is prime this means gcd(p,a) = gcd(a, p) = 1.
Since p|ab by the previous theorem we have p|b.
Corollary 1.11
If gcd(a,b) = 1 and a|c and b|c then ab|c.
Proof. If gcd(a,b) = 1 we have 1 = ax+by. Then c = acx+bcy. We have a|c i.e. c = aq. Then cb = abq
and thus bcy = ab(qy). The latter implies ab|bcy. We also have b|c i.e. c = br. Then ac = abr. Thus
acx = ab(rx). The latter implies ab|acx. Thus ab|bcy and ab|acx imply ab|acx+bcy. Therefore ab|c.
1.6.3
Relatively prime integers
Definition 1.14
For two integers a,b ∈Z if gcd(a,b) = 1 the two integers a and b are called relatively prime.

1.7
Least common multiples
Definition 1.15
Least common multiple :lcm
For a,b ∈Z we denote by lcm(a,b) the least common multiple of a and b, the least positive integer
divisible by both a,b.
For a,b ∈Z only the positive multiples are then considered.
Proposition 1.1
Let a,b ∈Z are such that |a|+|b| ̸= 0, and let n = lcm(a,b). If m is a common multiple of a,b show
that n|m.
This says that any common multiple of a,b is a multiple of the least common multiple (of a,b).
If T(a) is the set of (positive) multiples of a, then T(a) = T(−a) = T(|a|). Moreover T(a) ∩T(b) =
T(|a|)∩T(|b|). Therefore lcm(a,b) = lcm(|a|,|b|).
Proof. If one of a,b is equal to zero, then all multiples of zero is zero, common muliples can only be zero
and the statement is true trivially.
Otherwise divide m by n.
m = nq+r,0 ≤r < n.
We are given that m is a common multiple of a,b that is a|m and b|m. Moreover n is the lcm of a,b
that is a|n and b|n. Therefore a|r and b|r. Thus r is a common multiple of a,b. Moreover r < n and
the common multiple r is positive and LESS than the least common multiple a,b, a contradiction unless
r = 0. Then m = nq and thus n|m.
Moreover lcm(ma,mb) = mlcm(a,b).
Proposition 1.2
For a,b ∈Z we have lcm(am,bm) = mlcm(a,b).
Proof.
(a) Let X = lcm(a,b) and Y = lcm(am,bm). We are to show that Y = Xm. It suffices to show Y ≥Xm
and Y ≤Xm.
Since X = lcm(a,b) then a/X and b/X.
Therefore X = ad1 and X = bd2 for some d1,d2.
Conse-
quently Xm = mad1 and Xm = mbd2 respectively.
Furthermore ma|Xm and mb|Xm.
Thefore, since
Y = lcm(am,bm), we have Xm ≥Y.
Consider Y = lcm(am,bm). Them m|Y, and therefore there exists a y such that Y = my.
Y = lcm(am,bm) ⇒m|Y ⇒∃y : Y = my.
Combining the latter with Y = my we obtain
Y = my,Y = amd3,Y = bmd4 ⇒my = amd3,
my = bmd4 ⇒y = ad3,
y = bd4 ⇒a|y,
b|y,
⇒a|y,
b|y,⇒y ≥X ⇒my ≥mX ⇒Y ≥Xm.
From Xm ≥Y and Y ≥mX we conclude Y = Xm as needed.

Let a,b ∈N. If gcd(a,b) = 1 then lcm(a,b) = ab.

Corollary 1.13
If gcd(a,b,c) = 1 and gcd(a,b) = d > 1 then Equation 1.9 has no solution.
Proof. Say that Equation 1.9 has an integer solution (x,y) i.e. ax+by = c. Since d = gcd(a,b) it means
d|a and d|b. Then d|ax+by i.e. d|c. If d is a common divisor of a,b,c it means d|1. Then it can only
be d = 1. This contradicts the fact d > 1. Thus Equation 1.9 cannot have any solution.
Corollary 1.14
If gcd(a,b) = 1 then Equation 1.9 has one solution.

be d = 1. This contradicts the fact d > 1. Thus Equation 1.9 cannot have any solution. If gcd(a,b) = 1
then Equation 1.9 has one solution.

Theorem 1.16
For a, p ∈Z∗such that gcd(a, p) = 1, there is a unique x such that aa′ ≡1 (mod p).
Proof. Since gcd(a, p) = 1 we have by Theorem 1.10 that there exists x,y such that ax + py = 1 =
gcd(a, p). Furthermore −py = ax −1. Since p|py we have p|ax −1. Thus ax ≡1 (mod p). Consider
a′ = x (mod p). It is still aa′ ≡(mod p). Furthermore any divisor of a′ and m must also divide 1, i.e.
gcd(a′, p) = 1.

1.9
Fundamental theorem of arithmetic
1.9.1
Unique factorization
Theorem 1.17
Let n ∈N with n > 1. Then n can be expressed as the product of prime numbers (natural numbers
with two divisors one, and itself). The representation is unique up to a permutation of the ordering of
the primes.
We usually represent the prime numbers from the smallest (left-most side) to the largest (right-most
side). If a prime number p appears k times we also write pk. If n itself is a prime number the expression
”product” is used degeneratively. The product of one prime is the prime itself.
Proof.
We prove the result by induction: n > 1 becomes n ≥2.
Base case n = 2. Integer n = 2 is the degenerate case of n = 2 where there is only one prime factor of
n = 2 and this is itself.
Induction hypothesis: H(m), 2 ≤m < n. For n ≥2 we have that for every integer m such that 2 ≤m < n,
then m can be expressed as the products of primes as noted in the statement of the problem.
Inductive step: n Consider integer n. We distinguish two cases: (a) n is a prime number and we are
done, (b) n is not a prime number. In the latter case there exists by Proposition 1.3 a prime factor of
1 < p < n. Call then P = n/p. Then 1 < P < n. By the induction hypothesis P can be expressed as a
product of primes, and so does n, since n = P · p, where P is a product of primes then, and p is also a
prime.
Uniqueness of the representation. Let p1p2 ... pr is a representation and q1q2 ...qs is another repre-
sentation of n.
n = p1p2 ... pr−1pr = q1q2 ...qs−1qs,
and let 1 < p1 ≤p2 ≤... ≤pr, and 1 < q1 ≤q2 ≤... ≤qr, and furthermore 1 ≤r ≤s. By using induction
we will show that r = s and pi = qi for all i = 1,2,...,r(= s). Induction is on r.
Base case r = 1. We have
n = p1 = q1q2 ...qs.
On the left-hand side we have p1 a prime number. If s > 1 on the right-hand side we have the product
of at least two primes. This is a contradiction. Therefore s = 1. Then p1 = q1 and we are done.
Induction hypothesis. We assume that the result is true for r −1, where r ≥2. Consider the following.
Consider the following.
n = p1p2 ... pr−1pr = q1q2 ...qs−1qs,
pr is a factor of n. It also thus divides the q product. Since pr is a prime number and all qi are prime
numbers it can’t by that pr|qi and pr < qi because then qi would have a prime factor and could not be
a prime number itself. Thus the only way pr|qi is for pr = qi. In other words pr|qi implies pr ≤qi. We
might also work out similarly to show that qs ≤p j. Then
qs ≤p j ≤pr ≤qi ≤qs
implies pr = qs. Since both pr > 1,qs > 1 we divide the expression for n by pr = qs. We then obtain
p1p2 ... pr−1 = q1q2 ...qs−1,
and by the induction hypothesis r −1 = s−1 and p1 = q1, ..., pr−1 = qs−1.

For a prime number p if p|p1p2 then p|p1 or p|p2. This can be generalized for p|p1p2p3 ....

Theorem 1.18
Fundamental theorem of arithmetic
If n > 1 there there exists unique prime numbers p1 < ... < pk and natural integers a1,...,ak > 0 such
that
n = pa1

Proof. If n is prime this is true easily. Let n be a composite natural number. Then by Theorem 1.3 it
has a prime factor and let its smallest one be q1 i.e.
n = q1 ·a1,
with a1 < n.
If a1 is prime we have decomposed n into two prime numbers. If a1 is composite and by Theorem 1.3 it
has a prime factor and let its smallest one be q2 i.e.
a1 = q2 ·a2,
with a2 < a1.
n = q1a1 = q1(q2a2) = q1q2a2.
If a2 is composite we repeat this until he hit a ak−1 = qk. That way
n = q1q2 ...qk−1qk.
Let us assume that there is another factorization of n
n = r1r2 ...rt−1rt.
The we have
q1q2 ...qk−1qk = r1r2 ...rt−1rt.
r1 divides the right hand side. It also divides the left-hand side. By Lemma 1.6 one of qi divided by r1,

is r1. The smallest qi is q1. Thus r1 = q1. Because primes are ̸= 0, we can factor out r1.
q2 ...qk−1qk = r2 ...rt−1rt.
Continuing likewise if without loss of generality k < t we will eventually have
1 = rk+1 ...rt−1rt.
Prime numbers are > 1. Their product cannot be equal to 1. Thus this can only mean that k = t as
well.

1.9.4
Prime number theorem
Euclid’s theorem reproved.
Theorem 1.20
Infinitely many primes
There are infinitely many prime numbers distinct from each other.
Proof. Suppose that there are finitely many prime numbers i.e.
p1 < p2 < ... < pn
that is n distinct prime numbers exist. Then form the product N = p1p2... pn + 1. Since N > 1 by
Theorem 1.3 there is at least one prime p dividing N. This p cannot be any of the p1 ... pn. Why ? Say
p = pi for some i. Then p|N and p|p1 ... pn which would imply p|N −p1... pn. The latter implies p|1 i.e.
p ≤1 but given p is a prime number we must have p > 1. A contradiction. Thus p is a prime number
other than the ones of the finite group p1,..., pn.

1.9.5
Mersenne numbers
Definition 1.16
Mersenne numbers
The Mersenne number of order k is denoted as Mk = 2k −1, where k is a prime number.
Theorem 1.23
Mersenne prime numbers
If Mk = 2k −1 is a prime number, then Mk is a Mersenne prime (number).
Example 1.9.3
Find the first eight Mersenne numbers.
Solution. The first eight Mersenne numbers are as follows.
22 −1 = 3,
23 −1 = 7,
25 −1 = 31,
27 −1 = 127,
211 −1 = 2047,
213 −1 = 8191,
217 −1 = 131071,
219 −1 = 524287,

Example 1.9.4
Find the first five Mersenne prime numbers.
Solution. The first five Mersenne primes are as follows.
22 −1 = 3,
23 −1 = 7,
25 −1 = 31,
27 −1 = 127,
213 −1 = 8191,
Note that 2047 is missing from the second list. This is because 2047 = 23·89. One can verify 131071
and 524287 are both Mersenne primes as well.
1.9.6
Fermat numbers
Definition 1.17
Fermat numbers
The Fermat number of order k is denoted as Fk = 22k +1, k ≥0.
Theorem 1.24
Fermat prime numbers
If Fk is a prime number, then Fk is a prime Fermat number, otherwise it is a composite Fermat number.
Example 1.9.5
Find the first five Fermat numbers.
Solution.
F0 = 3,
F1 = 5,
F2 = 17,
F3 = 257,
F4 = 65537,
F5 = 4294967297.
All first give are prime Fermat numbers (to mychecking). But for the last one
4294967297 = 641∗6700417,
we conclude F5 is a composite Fermat number.
Example 1.9.6
Find the first five prime Fermat numbers.
Solution. Consider,
(Fn −1)2 +1 = (22n +1−1)2 +1 = 22·2n +1 = Fn+1.
Moreover
Fn+1 −2 = 22n+1 −1 = (22n +1)(22n −1) = Fn(Fn −2).
By induction
Fn+1 −2 = Fn ·(Fn −2) = Fn ·(Fn−1(Fn−1 −2)) = Fn ·Fn−1(Fn−1 −2) = FnFn−1 ...F0.
Therefore
Fk|Fn+1 −2,
0 ≤k ≤n.

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
1.10
A short review of algebra definitions
We recite some definitions. This section can be skipped as needed.
Definition 1.18
Equivalence relation
A binary relation R on X is an equivalence relation on X if for every x,y,z ∈X the following properties
are satisfied.
• (a) Reflexivity xRx for every x ∈X,
• (b) symmetry xRy then yRx, and
• (c) transitivity xRy and yRz then xRz.
Definition 1.19
Equivalence classes
In an equivalence relation we can decompose X into disjoint subsets known as equivalence classes.
Then for each x ∈X the set of elements equivalent to x define
[x/R] = {y ∈X : xRy}.
Then
p : X →X/R with p(x) = [x].
For an equivalence relation R if aRb the [a] = [b]. (It is the same as [a/R] = [b/R].)
Definition 1.20
Residue classes modulo n
Let n ∈N. The set of all residue classes modulo n or congruences mod n (or modulo n) is denoted in
simple form by Z/nZ.
Z/nZ = {0,1,2,...,n−1}.
This is supposed to mean the following.
Z/nZ = {(0)n,(1)n,(2)n,...,(n−1)n}.
We read this set “ Z modulo n ” or “ Z mod n ”. Sometimes we write Zn instead of Z/nZ. The
latter notation explains set Z is split by n into n equivalence classes. Two integer x,y belong to the same
equivalence class if (x−y) or (y−x) is a multiple of n. The equivalence class of x is x mod n.
1.10.1
Binary operators and operations
Definition 1.21
Binary operator
A binary operator defines a binary operation.
A binary operation is the operation implied by a binary operator.
Definition 1.22
Binary operation
For a set S and a binary operator ⊕, a binary operation is a rule that maps to each pair (x,y) ∈S×S
a unique z ∈S.
Operations +, ∗are binary operations for a set S = N or S = R. In all definitions below we assume S
is non-empty.


## Page 41


1.10. A SHORT REVIEW OF ALGEBRA DEFINITIONS
33
1.10.2
Groupoids and semigroups
Definition 1.23
Magma/Groupoid
Let S be a set, and ⊕be a closed operator S×S 7→S defining a total function as follows.
∀(x,y) ∈S×S : (x,y) 7→x⊕y ∈S.
The structure S equipped with ⊕as structured is a magma. We then say (S,⊕) defines a magma, also
known as a groupoid.
Operator ⊕, and when introduced operator ⊗, are closed operators S ×S 7→S defining a total function
as follows.
∀(x,y) ∈S×S : (x,y) 7→x⊕y ∈S.
This is also known as a closure property.
Definition 1.24
Semigroup
Let S be a non-empty set, and ⊕be an operator ⊕: S×S 7→S. If operator ⊕has the closure property
and is associative, then the structure S equipped with ⊕as structured is a semigroup.
1. Closure property: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊕y ∈S.
2. Associativity : (x⊕y)⊕z = x⊕(y⊕z)
∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S,∀z ∈S,
We then say equivalently that (S,⊕) defines a semigroup.
A monoid is a semigroup with an identity element.
Definition 1.25
Monoid
An (S,⊕) that is a semigroup it becomes a monoid if there exists an element e in S such that
∀x ∈S : e⊕x = x⊕e = x.
Then (S,⊕) is called a monoid.
A monoid equipped with an inverse (every element has an inverse element) is a group.
1.10.3
Groups
Definition 1.26
Group
An (S,⊕) that is a monoid it becomes a group if every element of S has an inverse element.
∀x ∈S,∃y ∈S : x⊕y = y⊕x = e,
where e is the identity element of the underlying monoid. Then (S,⊕) is called a group.
Below we give a standalone definition of a group.
Definition 1.27
Group
An (S,⊕) with S non-empty is a group if it has the following properties.
1. Closure property: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊕y ∈S.
2. Associativity : (x⊕y)⊕z = x⊕(y⊕z)
∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S,∀z ∈S,


## Page 42


34
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
3. Identity: there exists an e ∈S such that ∀x ∈S : e⊕x = x⊕e = x.
4. Inverse: ∀x ∈S,∃y ∈S : x⊕y = y⊕x = e.
Then (S,⊕) is called a group.
Example 1.10.1
For S = Z and ⊕= + we define e = 0. The inverse y of x is then denoted as −x. (Z,+) is a group. It
is also an Abelian group, defined next.
Definition 1.28
Commutative or Abelian group
An (S,⊕) that is a group becomes a commutative group if
∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊕y = y⊕x.
Then (S,⊕) is called a commutative group, also known as an Abelian group.
(R,+) is also a commutative (abelian) group.
Example 1.10.2
For S = R and ⊕= + we define e = 0 The inverse y of x is then denoted as −x. (Z,+) is a group.
Example 1.10.3
For an ⊕= + operator we use 0 as its identity element that is, e = 0. We denote the inverse of a as
−a. For an ⊕= ∗operator we use 1 as its identity element that is, e = 1. We denote the inverse of
an a ̸= 0 as a−1 or 1/a.
A group with operator ⊕= + is known as an additive group. A group with operator ⊕= ∗is known
as a multiplicative group.
Definition 1.29
Order of a group
The order of a group (S,⊕) is the number of its elements, and it is denoted |S| (cardinality symbol).
Definition 1.30
Subgroup
For a group (S,⊕) and a non-empty subset T of S that is T ⊆S, we say T a sugroup of S if (T,⊕) is
a group.
The order of a group is the cardinality of its underlying set S.
(R,+) is also an abelian group. (Z+,+) is not an abelian group. There is no identity element then.
(Z+,∗) is not an abelian group. There is an identity element then which is 1, but there is no inverse for
most elements (other than one)! (Q+,∗) is an abelian group. (R+,∗) is an abelian group. (Q∗,∗) is an
abelian group. (R∗,∗) is an abelian group.
Example 1.10.4
For S = R−{0} = Rx and ⊕= · we define e = 1 The inverse y of x is then denoted as 1/x. (Rx,·) is a
group.
(Rx,·) is also an abelian group.


## Page 43


1.10. A SHORT REVIEW OF ALGEBRA DEFINITIONS
35
Definition 1.31
Cyclic group
For a group (S,⊗) and a ∈S, the elements ai, where i is an integer, form a subgroup of S, called the
subgroup generated by a. A group is cyclic if there is an element a ∈S that the subgroup generated
by a is S itself. We can the write S as follows.
S = {e,a,a2,...,ak−1},
where e is the identity of the group, and k is the smallest (positive) integer such that ak = e.
If S is infinite,
S = {...a−2,a−1,e,a,a2,...},
1.10.4
Rings and Integral domains
Definition 1.32
Ring
A ring is an algebraic structure consisting of a set S in which two binary closed operations are defined
say ⊕,⊗. Then, (S,⊕,⊗) is a ring if (a) (S,⊕) is an abelian group, (b) (S,⊗) is a semigroup, and (c)
⊗is distributive over ⊕, with e being the identity element of (S,⊕). A ring denoted as (S,⊕,⊗) has
the following properties.
1. Closure property for ⊕: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊕y ∈S.
2. Associativity for ⊕: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S,∀z ∈S,(x⊕y)⊕z = x⊕(y⊕z)
3. Commutativity for ⊕: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊕y = y⊕x.
4. Identity for ⊕: there exists an e = 0 ∈S such that ∀x ∈S : 0⊕x = x⊕0 = x.
5. Additive inverse for ⊕: ∀x ∈S,∃y = −x ∈S : x⊕y = y⊕x = e.
6. Closure property for ⊗: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊗y ∈S.
7. Associativity for ⊗: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S,∀z ∈S,(x⊗y)⊗z = x⊗(y⊗z).
8. Distribution of ⊗with respect to ⊕:
∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S,∀z ∈S : x⊗(y⊕z) = (x⊗y)⊕(x⊗z)∧(x⊕y)⊗z = (x⊗z)⊕(y⊗z).
Then (S,⊕,⊗) is called a ring.
All of Z,Q,R,C are rings; in fact they are commutative rings for ⊗being commutative as well. Non-
commutative rings are n×n matrices whose elements are from the rings above.
Definition 1.33
Commutative ring
(S,⊕,⊗) is called a commutative ring if (S,⊕,⊗) is a ring and ⊗is also commutative.
∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊗y = y⊗x.
Definition 1.34
Ring with identity
(S,⊕,⊗) is called a ring with identity if (S,⊕,⊗) is a ring and ⊗supports an identity element let us
call it 1.
Identity for ⊗: ∃e = 1 ∈S : ∀x ∈S : 1⊗x = x⊗1 = x.

CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
Definition 1.35
Integral domain
Let (S,⊕,×) be a non-zero commutative ring with 0 = e ̸= 1, where e or 0 is the identity of ⊕and 1
is the identity of ⊗, that has no zero divisors which is equivalent to the following.
a ̸= 0,b ̸= 0 ⇒c = ab ̸= 0 or equivalently c = ab = 0 ⇒a = 0∨b = 0.
Then the structure (S,⊕,⊗) is called an integral domain.
Definition 1.36
Division ring
A division ring (S,⊕,⊗) is a ring with identity 1 ̸= 0 with an inverse element over ⊗, that is
∀x ∈S,x ̸= 0,∃a ∈S : ax = xa = 1.
Definition 1.37
Ideal
For a ring (S,⊕,⊗), a left ideal I of S is a subset of S that is a subgroup of the structure (S,⊕) of the
ring that is closed under left multiplication over elements of S. Thefore e ∈I, e = 0 being the identity
of (S,⊕), and for every a,b ∈I and x ∈S the following are true.
a+b ∈I,
−a ∈I,
xa ∈I.
A right ideal is defined similarly. A two-sided ideal is a left and right ideal; oftentimes it is called an
ideal. If the ring is commutative, then the left, right, and two-sided ideals coincide and the term ideal
identifies all three of them. Then the ideal I is an abelian subgroup.
1.10.5
Fields
A field can be defined as an extension of a ring as follows.
Definition 1.38
Field from ring
A field (S,⊕,⊗) is a division ring that supports a commutative ⊗.
∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊗y = y⊗x.
A field can also be defined as an extension of two groups as follows.
Definition 1.39
Field from groups
For a field (S,⊕,⊗), (S,⊕) is an abelian group, (S,⊗) is an abelian group but with an inverse defined
only for S−{0}. Moreover ⊗is distributive with respect to ⊕.
An equivalent standalone definition of a field follows.
Definition 1.40
Field
(S,⊕,⊗) is a field with two binary operators ⊕,⊗if S has at least two elements, there exist 1 ̸= 0
where 0 is the identity of ⊕, and 1 is the identity of ⊗and it supports the following properties.
1. Closure property for ⊕: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊕y ∈S.
2. Associativity for ⊕: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S,∀z ∈S,(x⊕y)⊕z = x⊕(y⊕z)
3. Commutativity for ⊕: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊕y = y⊕x.

4. (Additive) Identity for ⊕: there exists an e = 0 ∈S such that ∀x ∈S : 0⊕x = x⊕0 = x.
5. Additive inverse for ⊕: ∀x ∈S,∃y = −x ∈S : x⊕y = y⊕x = e.
6. Closure property for ⊗: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊗y ∈S.
7. Associativity for ⊗: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S,∀z ∈S,(x⊗y)⊗z = x⊗(y⊗z).
8. Commutativity for ⊗: ∀x ∈S,∀y ∈S : x⊗y = y⊗x.

(x⊕y)⊗z = (x⊗z)⊕(y⊗z).
Then (S,⊕,⊗) is called a field.
Roughly speaking for a field (S,⊕,⊗), (S,⊕) is an abelian group, (S,⊗) is short of an abelian group,
with “short” defined that only elements x ̸= e i.e. x ̸= 0 have a multiplicative inverse y = 1/x = x−1, that
is, (S−{e},⊗) is a group.
Definition 1.41
Finite field
A finite field is a field that has a finite number of elements or equivalently |S| < ∞. The order of a field
is the number of its elements.
Theorem 1.25
Galois
Finite fields exists for an order q that is a prime power that is q = pk, where k ≥1.
A finite field is also called a Galois field and denoted GF(q).

1.11
Modular arithmetic
Note 1.2
The terms congruence and residue, congruence class and residue class will be used interchangeably in
the remainder.
Definition 1.42
Congruence modulo n
Let n ∈Z∗
+. For a,b ∈Z we say a ≡b (mod n) if n/(a −b), or equivalently, if the remainder of the
division of a by n is equal to the remainder of the division of b by n.
∀a,b ∈Z
a ≡b
(mod n)
⇔
n|a−b.
Congruence modulo n and congruence mod n mean the same thing. We can read a ≡b (mod n) by
saying that ”a is congruent to b modulo n”. Then the ”difference of a and b is a multiple of n” or ”n
divides the difference of a and b”. Note that several times the ≡is replaced by = and the parentheses
around the mod are dropped.
Proposition 1.3
Modular division
Let n ∈N and n > 1 and let a ∈Z. Then there exists a unique integer 0 ≤r < n such that
a ≡r
(mod n).
Proof. If n > 1 and a ∈Z division (Theorem 1.5) implies that there are unique q,r such that a = nq+r,
with 0 ≤r < |n|. If in addition n is positive (e.g. n > 1), then 0 ≤r < n. Then a −r = nq i.e. a ≡r
(mod n).
Definition 1.43
Least residue
If a ≡b (mod n) we refer to b as the residue of a modulo n. For a b such that 0 ≤b < n, we refer to
b as the least non-negative residue of a modulo n.
Note 1.3
Oftentimes the least non-negative residue of a modulo n is denoted as a mod n. Therefore
a ≡b
(mod n) ⇔a mod n = b mod n.
Lemma 1.7
Let n ∈Z+, and a,b ∈Z. The following conditions are equivalent.
1. a ≡b (mod n),
2. a = b+nk, for some k ∈Z, and
3. n|(a−b).
Proof.
1. Since a ≡b (mod n), there exist a1,b1,m ∈Z such as: a = a1n +m, b = b1n +m, where 0 ≤m < n.
Then, a−b = (a1 −a2)n i.e. a = b+(a1 −a2)n and therefore a = b+nk, where k = (a1 −a2), thus proving
2. Furthermore, a−b = (a1 −a2)n is by definition equivalent to n|(a−b) thus proving 3.


## Page 47


1.11. MODULAR ARITHMETIC
39
2. Let a = b + nk for some k. Let b = nq + r for some 0 ≤r < n. The a = nk + nq + r = n(k + q) + r.
Therefore a ≡b (mod n) thus proving 1. Moreover a = b+nk implies a−b = nk which is equivalent to
n|(a−b) thus proving 3.
3. Let n|(a−b). This mean a−b = nk for some integer k. This is 2, and from 2 we might derive 1 as
well.
Note that the | (divisible/divisibility relation) is reflexive and transitive but not symmetric, and thus
it is not an equivalence relation. In fact if a|b and b|a we have |a| = |b| or for positive integers a = b.
Corollary 1.18
Properties of modular arithmetic
Let n ∈N with n > 1. The modn operation has the following properties.
1. Reflexive a ≡a (mod n).
2. Symmetric a ≡b (mod n) ⇐⇒b ≡a (mod n).
3. Transitive If a ≡b (mod n) and b ≡c (mod n), then a ≡c (mod n).
4. Translation If a ≡b (mod n) then for any integer c, then a+c ≡b+c (mod n).
5. Scaling If a ≡b (mod n) then for any integer c, then a·c ≡b·c (mod n).
6. Additivity If a1 ≡b1 (mod n) and a2 ≡b2 (mod n), then a1 +a2 ≡b1 +b2 (mod n).
7. Subtractivity If a1 ≡b1 (mod n) and a2 ≡b2 (mod n), then a1 −a2 ≡b1 −b2 (mod n).
8. Multiplicativity If a1 ≡b1 (mod n) and a2 ≡b2 (mod n), then a1 ·a2 ≡b1 ·b2 (mod n).
9. Exponentiativity If a ≡b (mod n) then for any integer c > 0, then ac ≡bc (mod n).
Proof.
Properties (1)-(3) follow from the proof of Lemma 1.7.
(1) For n|a−a i.e. n|0 obviously.
(2) If n|a−b then n|b−a obviously.
(3) If n|a−b and n|b−c we have respectively a−b = kn and b−c = mn for some k,m ∈Z. Adding the
two together we get a−c = (k +m)n i.e. n|a−c i.e. a ≡c (mod n).
(4) The translation property follows similarly. If a ≡b (mod n) then a = kn+b. Then (a+c) = kn+(b+c)
or (a−c) = kn+(b−c) and the result follows.
(5) The scaling property follows similarly. If a ≡b (mod n) then a = kn+b. Then (a·c) = (kn+b)·c or
ac = (kc)n+bc and the result follows.
(6)-(7) They follow similarly to (4)-(5). If a1 ≡b1 (mod n) then a1 = kn+b1. If a2 ≡b2 (mod n) then
a2 = ln+b2. Then, a1 +a2 ≡b1 +b2 (mod n), or a1 −a2 ≡b1 −b2 (mod n),
(8) Moreover, a1a2 = (kb2 +lb1 +kln)n+b1b2 and thus a1 ·a2 ≡b1 ·b2 (mod n).
(9) For a ≡b (mod n) implying a = kn+b, we use the binomial theorem to show that
ac = (kn+b)c ≡bc
(mod n),
as needed. Or it can follow by induction and the scaling property (4). The result then follows.
A note on division. Operation modn was used in the context of modular addition, subtraction and
multiplication. We have been silent about division. This is because of the following. 4 ̸≡2 (mod 6) yet
3·4 ≡3·2 (mod 6). Also 2·3 ≡0 (mod 6) yet 2 ̸≡0 (mod 6) and 3 ̸≡0 (mod 6).

Theorem 1.26
Cancellation law
If ac ≡bc (mod n), and gcd(n,c) = 1 then a ≡b (mod n).
Proof. It is ac + qn = bc + rn i.e. ac −bc = sn for s = r −q for some integer r,q. Thus n|ac −bc or
n|(a−b)c. Since gcd(n,c) = 1, we have that n|a−b. This implies a ≡b (mod n).

Theorem 1.31
The modular equation
ax ≡1
(mod n)
has a solution if and only if gcd(a,n) = 1.
Proof. The gcd(a,n)|b of the previous theorem for b = 1 becomes gcd(a,n)|1. Thus gcd(a,n) ≤1. The
gcd is always a positive integer i.e. gcd(a,n) ≥1. Thus gcd(a,n) = 1 as needed.

Theorem 1.32
The modular equation, for prime p,
ax ≡1
(mod p)
has a solution for x if p ∤a.
Proof. By the previous theorem for a solution to exist gcd(a, p) = 1. Since p is a prime its only positive
divisors are 1 and p. Given that p cannot divide a, we have that the gcd(a, p) = 1. The result follows.
Equivalently, it can be stated as follows.
Theorem 1.33
If p is a prime number, the modular equation, for prime p and p ∤a, then there exists an x such that
1 ≤x ≤p−1 such that the modular equation,
ax ≡1
(mod p)
has a solution for x. The x is sometimes denoted as the inverse of a modulo p i.e. a−1.
The a is called a unit modulo p, as it has an inverse. An a that is not a unit is called a zero divisor
modulo p.
Definition 1.44
The a such that a·a−1 ≡1 (mod p) is called a unit modulo n, as it has an inverse.
Definition 1.45
The a such that there does not exist an a−1 such that a · a−1 ≡1 (mod p) is called a zero divisor
modulo p.
Theorem 1.34
Let n > 1 be an integer and n ∤a. The following are equivalent.
(a) a is a zero divisor (mod n),
(b) a has no inverse (mod n),
(c) there exists a x ∈Z such that n ∤x and ax ≡0 (mod n).
Proof. Statements (a) and (b) are true and equivalent by the prior definition and introduction of unit
and zero divisor.
Suppose that (b) is true and a has no inverse. By Theorem 1.31 gcd(a,n) > 1. Let gcd(a,n) = d > 1.
The a = dr and n = ds for some integer r,s.
For 1 << z < n we have z ̸≡(mod n).
Furthermore,
as = (dr)s = (ds)r = nr ≡0 (mod n). Statement (c) follows from Statement (b).
Suppose that statement (c) is true. There there exists an s ∈Z such that n ∤a and as ≡0 (mod n).
We are going to prove a has no inverse. Let us assume that a has an inverse, then aa−1 ≡1 (mod n),
and then
0 ≡as ≡asa−1 ≡(aa−1)s ≡s
(mod n).
The latter implies that n|s that contradicts the assumption that n ∤s! Thus a has no inverse and statement
(b) is true coming from (c). Thus statements (b) and (c) are equivalent.

1.12
Residue classes or congruence classes
Note that the | (divisible/divisibility relation) is reflexive and transitive but not symmetric, and thus it is
not an equivalence relation. In fact if a|b and b|a we have |a| = |b| or for positive integers a = b.
Lemma 1.8
Congruence is an equivalence relation
The congruence modn is an equivalence relation: it is reflexive, symmetric and transitive on Z.
Proof.
1. It is straightforward to show modn is reflexive. For every a we have a ≡a mod n since a−a = 0·n.
2. It is also symmetric since if a ≡b (mod n) this implies a−b = nk for some integer k. Then b−a = n(−k)
and therefore b ≡a (mod n).
3. Furthermore, a ≡b (mod n) and b ≡c (mod n) imply a−b = kn and b−c = ln for some integers k,l.
Therefore adding up the equalities we obtain a −c = (k +l)n anf therefore a ≡c (mod n) thus proving
transitivity.
Definition 1.46
Congruence or residue class
The residue class or congruence class of a mod n or a modulo n is denoted as (a)n.
Definition 1.47
Congruence or residue classes
Let n ∈Z+. We define classes (a)n (or [a]n), for all a = 0,1,...,n−1, a ∈Z.
(a)n = {b ∈Z|b ≡a
(mod n)} = {b = a+kn|k ∈Z}.
(0)n,(1)n,...,(n −1)n are the n equivalence class modulo n. Every integer b belongs to one of those
classes depending on its remainder after a division with n. Thus b ∈(a)n if and only if b ≡a (mod n).
One might use (a)n or [a]n, or a+nZ, or a mod n to denote the equivalence class a modulo (or mod)
n. Arithmetic on equivalence classes is known as modular arithmetic.


## Page 54


46
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
Example 1.12.1
Integers mod 3 can fall into three equivalence classes. Describe them.
Solution.
(0)3 = {...,−3,0,+3,+6,...},
then
(1)3 = {...,−2,1,+4,+7,...},
and finally,
(2)3 = {...,−1,2,+5,+8,...}.
Integers 5 and 8 belong to (2)3 because the remainder of the integer division of 5 by 3 is a 2. And so is
the remainder of the division of 8 by 3.
Definition 1.48
Equivalence classes modn
Let Zn or Z/nZ be the set of equivalence classes of integers modulo n. That is,
Z/nZ = Zn = {(0)n,(1)n,...,(n−1)n}.
If a ∈(k)n then a = nq+k i.e. a ≡k (mod n). If b ∈(m)n then b = nr +m i.e. b ≡m (mod n). Then
(a + b) ∈(k + m)n. Naturally (k + m)n is ((k + m) (mod n))n. Moreover (ab) ∈(km)n. We can then
define operations on the elements of Zn as follows.
1. (Addition) (k)n +(m)n = (k +m)n, or (k +nZ)+(m+nZ) = (k +m)+nZ.
2. (Multiplication) (k)n(m)n = (km)n, or (k +nZ)·(m+nZ) = (k ·m)+nZ.
3. (Additivity) If (a1)n = (b1)n and (a2)n = (b2)n then (a1 +a2)n = (b1 +b2)n.
4. (Multiplicativity) If (a1)n = (b1)n and (a2)n = (b2)n then (a1 ·a2)n = (b1 ·b2)n.
5. (Exponentiativity) If (a1)n = (b1)n and c ∈Z+, (ac
1)n = (bc
1)n.
Note 1.4
Several times, instead of using the correct notation
Z/nZ = Zn = {(0)n,(1)n,...,(n−1)n},
one can use a simplified notation, as follows.
Z/nZ = Zn = {0,1,...,n−1}.
Thus we won’t use the correct notation (5)7 +(3)7 = (1)7. Instead we will write 5+3 ≡1 (mod 7).
Theorem 1.36
For a positive integer n,
1. (a)n = (b)n if and only if a ≡b (mod n),
2. For 0 ≤a,b < n either (a)n = (b)n or (a)n ̸= (b)n,
3. There are exaclty n residue class mod n and no more and they cover all of Z.


## Page 55


1.12. RESIDUE CLASSES OR CONGRUENCE CLASSES
47
Proof.
(1) Consider (a)n = (b)n. Thus for an element x x ∈(a)n if and only if x ∈(b)n. If x ∈(a)n then x = nk+a.
If x ∈(b)n then x = nl +b for some integer k,l. Therefore nk +a = nl +b which leads to a−b = n(l −k)
and therefore a ≡b (mod n). Consider x ∈(a)n. Then x ≡a (mod n). Since a ≡b (mod n) by transitivity
x ≡b (mod n), and thus x ∈(b)n. Therefore (a)n ⊆(b)n. Similarly we show (b)n ⊆(a)n, and the result
follows.
(2) Consider a,b. If a ≡b (mod n) then (a)n = (b)n from part (1). Otherwise a ̸≡(mod n). Then
(a)n ̸= (b)n. If this was not the case, then there would exist x ∈(a)n ∩(b)n. Arguments similar to the
proof in (1) then lead to a ≡b (mod n), a contradiction.
(3) Consider integer a ∈min{Z} and take the integer division of a by n: a = nq+r where q is the quotient
and r the remainder, where 0 ≤r < n. Then a ≡r (mod n), and thus a ∈(r)n. For 0 ≤i, j < n, i ̸= j we
have i ̸≡j (mod n). Thus those n congruence classes ((0)n,...(n−1)n divide Z.
Example 1.12.2
Ring Z/6Z is not an integral domain. Consider 2 ̸≡0 (mod 6). Consider 3 ̸≡0 (mod 6). Then 2·3 ≡0
(mod 6).
Example 1.12.3
6Z is an ideal I (the set of multiples of 6). Then Z/6Z is the set of cosets
{a+I : a ∈Z},
where operations +,· are defined as follows.
(a+I)+(b+I) = (a+b)+I,
(a+I)·(b+I) = (a·b)+I.
Two elements a,b are equivalent a ≡b or equivalently a+I = b+I if a−b ∈I. If Z is a ring, Z/6Z is
a quotient ring also known as a factor ring.
1.12.1
Complete system of congruences
Definition 1.49
Complete system of congruences
Let n ∈Z+, and A be a set of integers. A is a complete set of congruences mod n if and only if
• |A| = n, and
• A contains exactly one element from each one of the n congruence classes mod n.
Example 1.12.4
Let n = 3. Then A = {9,4,5} is a complete system of congruences (residues). Note 9 ≡0 (mod 3),
4 ≡1 (mod 3), and 5 ≡2 (mod 3).
Theorem 1.37
Let n ∈Z+, and A be a set of integers. A is a complete set of congruences mod n if and only if
• |A| = n, and
• no two elements of A are congruent mod n.


## Page 56


48
CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTORY NUMBER THEORY
Proof.
⇒. If A is a complete set of congruences mod n the two conditions above are obviously true.
⇐. If no two elements of A are congruent mod n, then they belong to different congruence classes mod
n. Given that A has n elements all the congruence classes are represented in A and thus A is a complete
system of congruences.
1.12.2
Reduced system of congruences
A congruence class (b)n is relatively prime to n if and only if gcd(b,n) = 1.

Let n ∈Z+.Then φ(n) is the number of congruence classes modulo n which are relatively prime to n.
Then the set of integers

a1,a2,...,aφ(n)
	
defines a reduced system of congruences if the set contains exactly one element from each congruence
class mod n that is relatively prime to n.
Definition 1.51
Reduced system of congruences
Let n ∈Z+, then φ(n) is the number of congruence classes modulo n which are relatively prime to n.
A congruence class (b)n is relatively prime to n if and only if gcd(b,n) = 1. Then the set of integers

a1,a2,...,aφ(n)
	
defines a reduced system of congruences if the set contains exactly one element from each congruence
class mod n that is relatively prime to n.

Theorem 1.38
Let n ∈Z+. Let A be a set of integers. Then A is a reduced system of congruences mod n if and only
if
1. A has exactly φ(n) elements,
2. no two elements a,b are congruent mod n (i.e. a ̸≡b (mod n)
,∀a,b ∈A)
3. each element of A is relatively prime to n (i.e. ∀a ∈A,
gcd(a,n) = 1).
Proof. A reduced set of congruences possesses those properties by default. Let us prove the converse.
Let A be a set of integers having those three properties. Because no two elements of A are congruent
mod n, it means they belong to difference congruence classes. All classes of A’s elements are relatively

all φ(n) classes whose elements are relatively prime to n. Thus A is a reduced set of congruences.

1.12.4
Congruence summary
Proposition 1.5
The set Z/nZ of integers mod n satisfies the following properties over addition.
1. Closure property: (i)n +(j)n ∈Z/nZ
∀(i)n,(j)n ∈Z/nZ.
2. Associativity property: ((i)n +(j)n)+(k)n = (i)n +((j)n +(k)n)
∀(i)n,(j)n,(k)n ∈Z/nZ.
3. Commutativity property: (i)n +(j)n = (j)n +(i)n
∀(i)n,(j)n ∈Z/nZ.
4. Identity that is (0)n, ∀(i)n ∈Z/nZ : (i)n +(0)n = (0)n +(i)n = (i)n.
5. (Additive) inverse property: ∀(i)n ∈Z/nZ
∃(j)n ∈Z/nZ : (i)n +(j)n = (j)n +(i)n = (0)n. More-
over (j)n = (−i)n = −(i)n.
Proposition 1.6
The set Z/nZ of integers mod n satisfies the following properties over multiplication.
1. Closure property: (i)n ·(j)n ∈Z/nZ
∀(i)n,(j)n ∈Z/nZ.
2. Associativity property: ((i)n ·(j)n)·(k)n = (i)n ·((j)n ·(k)n)
∀(i)n,(j)n,(k)n ∈Z/nZ.
3. Commutativity property: (i)n ·(j)n = (j)n ·(i)n
∀(i)n,(j)n ∈Z/nZ.
4. Identity that is (1)n, ∀(i)n ∈Z/nZ : (i)n ·(1)n = (1)n ·(i)n = (i)n.
5. Distribution property of multiplication over addition: (i)n·((j)n+(k)n) = ((i)n·(j)n)+((i)n·(k)n).
Therefore (Z/nZ,+,·) is a ring, since (Z/nZ,+) is an abelian group, (Z/nZ,·) is a semigroup, and · is
distributive over +. Furthermore, · is commutative thus making (Z/nZ,+,·) a commutative ring.
Proposition 1.7
Zn or Z/nZ is a commutative ring.
Zn or Z/nZ is such that (Z/nZ,+,·) is a commutative ring.
Proposition 1.8
The ring (Z/nZ,+,·) is a field for a prime integer n.

Lemma 2.1
If p,q are prime numbers and then gcd(p,q) = 1, then
φ(pq) = φ(p)φ(q).
Proof. The number of integers relatively prime to pq are the multiples of p, the multiple of q, and if
we include in this count pq make sure it is not included twice. Thus out of the pq integers 1,2,..., pq.
There are pq/p = q multiples of p (including pq) and there are pq/q = p multiples of q (including pq),
and thus the number of integers relatively prive to pq are
pq−p−q+1,
where the +1 is because in −p and −q the contribution of pq was subracted twice and we adjust with
a +1 accordingly. Then
pq−p−q+1 = (p−1)(q−1) = φ(p)φ(q).

2.2
Units
Let n be a positive integer. The set of all residue classes mod n is denoted as Z/nZ, and rarely Zn or
Z/n. It is the set of integers modulo n, thus representing the n equivalence classes that the integers of Z
can be split into depending on the remainder of their division by n. Zn is a cyclic group under addition,
and a commutative ring under multiplication and addition. The ring is a field for a prime n.

Definition 2.2
Units in Z
A divisor of one is called a unit. The only units of Z are +1 and −1.
Definition 2.3
Alternative definition of a unit in Z
An element a in Z that has an inverse in Z is also known as a unit. Only +1 and −1 have inverses in
Z.
In Z, +1 or −1 are indeed the only invertible elements of Z.
Proposition 2.2
There are no other units in Z, other than +1 and −1.
Proof. Let u be a third unit in Z with u ̸= ±1. Then 1 = uq for some integer q ∈Z. Since u ̸= ±1 and
integer |u| ≥2, noting 1 = uq implies u ̸= 0 as well. But |u| ≥2 and uq = 1 implies |q| ≤1/2 and thus
q = 0. Then uq = 0, a contradiction to uq = 1.
2.2.2
Inverses
Definition 2.4
Let n ∈Z+ and consider Z/nZ (also known as Zn). Let a ∈Zn. The inverse b of a mod n is defined as
b·a ≡1
(mod n).
We can then say that a and b are multiplicative inverses of one another.
We write b as 1/a or a−1.
Note that implicit in this definition is that a,b ∈Z/nZ or equivalently
1 ≤a,b < n. We might use in the remainder for simplicity Zn for Z/nZ.
Proposition 2.3
The inverse of a mod n exists if and only if gcd(a,n) = 1.
This is a byproduct of the extended GCD or results derived for a diophantine equation discussion. We
can prove then a more general result.
Corollary 2.4
Let n > 1 be an integer and n ∤a. The following are
(a) a is a zero divisor (mod n),
(b) a has no inverse (mod n),
(c) there exists a s ∈Z such that n ∤s and as ≡0 (mod n).
Proof. Statements (a) and (b) are true and equivalent by the prior definition and introduction of unit
and zero divisor.
Suppose that (b) is true and a has no inverse. By Theorem 1.31 gcd(a,n) > 1. Let gcd(a,n) = d > 1.
The a = dr and n = ds for some integer r,s.
For 1 << z < n we have z ̸≡(mod n).
Furthermore,
as = (dr)s = (ds)r = nr ≡0 (mod n). Statement (c) follows from Statement (b).
Suppose that statement (c) is true. There there exists an s ∈Z such that n ∤a and as ≡0 (mod n). We
are going to prove a has no inverse. Let us assume that a has an inverse, then aa−1 ≡1 (mod n), and
then
0 ≡as ≡asa−1 ≡(aa−1)s ≡s
(mod n).

56
CHAPTER 2. INTERMEDIATE NUMBER THEORY
The latter implies that n|s that contradicts the assumption that n ∤s! Thus a has no inverse and statement
(b) is true coming from (c). Thus statements (b) and (c) are equivalent. For prime p, ab ≡0 (mod p)
implies either a ≡0 (mod p) or b ≡0 (mod p). Thus for a prime p there no zero divisors other than 0
(mod p).
Proposition 2.4
There are φ(n) numbers a in Zn for which the inverse a−1 = 1/a exists.
This is a consequence of the fact that there are φ(n) natural numbers less that n that are relatively
prime to n.
Example 2.2.1
For n = 10 there are only φ(10) = 4, a’s with inverses. These are a = 1,3,7,9 with a−1 = 1,7,3,9
respectively.
Theorem 2.3
The ring (Z/nZ,+,·) is a field if and only if n is a prime number.
Proof. From Proposition (1.5) we have that Z/nZ is an abelian group over +. From Proposition (1.5)
we have that Z/nZ minus the 0 (additive identity) is an abelian group over ·, if a (multiplicative) inverse
property is true i.e. ∀(i)n ∈Z/nZ
∃(j)n ∈Z/nZ : (i)n ·(j)n = (j)n ·(i)n = (1)n. Moreover (j)n = 1/(i)n =
(i)−1
n . For the latter to be the case n must be a prime number. (This is derived from prior results.)
2.2.3
Units in Z/nZ
Consider Z/nZ also known as Zn. Any g ∈Z/nZ that is invertible and thus gx ≡1 (mod n) has a solution
for x, the inverse of g moduloe n, is known as a unit of Zn and thus of Z/nZ. We further conclude that
g is a unit if and only if gcd(g,n) = 1. Then gx ≡1 (mod n) has a solution for x = g−1.
Definition 2.5
Units in Z/nZ
Any g ∈Z/nZ that has an inverse modulo n, that is, there exists an x that satisfies the following
modular equation
gx ≡1
(mod n),
is called a unit modulo n. The x is also known as the inverse of g modulo n. Moreover gcd(g,n) = 1.
Definition 2.6
Zero divisors in Zn
An g that is not a unit is called a zero divisor modulo n.
Definition 2.7
Set of units Un
The set of units of Zn is denoted as Un. It is also denoted as Zx
n. It is also denoted as (Z/nZ)x. It is
also denoted as Z∗
n.
Proposition 2.5
Let m,n > 1 be integer. The following two statements are equivalent.
(i) a is a unit (mod mn).
(ii) a is a unit (mod m) and a is a unit (mod n).


## Page 65


2.2. UNITS
57
Proof.
(i) ⇒(ii). If a is a unit
(mod mn) then it means gcd(a,mn) = 1. We claim that this implies that
gcd(a,m) = gcd(a,n) = 1. If this was not so, and say gcd(a,m) = d > 1, then d becomes a common
divisor of a and m (and consequently of of mn as well). This would imply gcd(a,mn) > 1, a contradiction
to gcd(a,mn) = 1.
Then gcd(a,m) = gcd(a,n) = 1. Therefore ax ≡1 (mod m) has a solution for x and thus a is unit mod
m. Likewise, ay ≡1 (mod n) has a solution for y and thus a is unit mod n. Case completed.
(ii) ⇒(i). If a is a unit mod m then gcd(a,m) = 1. If a is a unit mod n then gcd(a,n) = 1. The former
imply gcd(a,mn) = 1.
If the latter was not so, then gcd(a,mn) = d > 1. There is a prime factor p of d i.e. p|d. Then p|a and
thus p|mn. The latter implies p|m or p|n. One or the other combined with p|a implies that p|gcd(a,m)
or p|gcd(a,n) contradicting that gcd(a,m) = gcd(a,n) = 1. The result then follows.
2.2.4
The totient function as cardinality of a set
Definition 2.8
Cardinality of Un
The set of units Un has cardinality φ(n), where φ(n) is Euler’s totient function.
Let p be a prime. Every non-zero element of Zp i.e. 1,..., p −1 is relatively prime to p, and thus
φ(p) = p−1. Moreover |Up| = φ(p) = p−1.
2.2.5
Units of rings
Definition 2.9
Set of units of ring S is Sx
Let (S,+,·) be a ring, and let x ∈S. We say that x is invertible or equivalently that x is a unit, if there
exists a y ∈S such that
x·y = y·x = 1,
where the multiplication · implies the multiplicative operation of S, and 1 is the identity element of S
over ·. The set of units of S is denoted as Sx.
Note that for x, the y such that x·y = y·x = 1 is uniquely defined. For there was a z such that x·z = z·x = 1,
then y = y(xz) = (yx)z = z. The unique y that is the inverse of x is sometimes denoted as x−1.
Proposition 2.6
For a ring (S,+,·), we have that (Sx,·) is a group.
Definition 2.10
Division ring
Let (S,+,·) be a ring. If all of its non-zero elements are invertible, then S is a division ring. Moreover,
if S is commutative then S is a field.
Lemma 2.4
If a ∈Z/nZ is a unit modulo n then gcd(a,n) = 1.

CHAPTER 2. INTERMEDIATE NUMBER THEORY
2.3
Chinese remainder theorem
Example 2.3.1
A farmer has some pounds sugar. If the farmer puts them into bags of 11 pounds the farmer can fit
enough full bags but then is left with 2 spare pounds. If the farmer uses 20 pound bags then is also
left with 1 spare pound. How many pounds of sugar does the farmer have?
Say the farmer has 321 pounds of sugar. This amount needs 29 11-pound bags and there 2 spare pounds.
If the farmer uses 20-pound bags the farmer can fill 16 bags and is left with 1 pound. Is it a solution.
Example 2.3.2
What if the farmer has 101 pounds of sugar ? 101 = 11·9+2 = 5·20+1.
The only solution in 0...219 seems to be 101. But beyond that range, another solution is 101+220,
100+2·220, and so on.
Theorem 2.4

unique A (mod n1 ...nk) such that it satisfies all of the modular equation below.

where N = n1n2 ...nk.
Proof. Let Nj, for j = 1,...,k contain all ni except n j. That is
Nj = n1 ...nj−1nj+1 ...nk.
We have ni|Nj for all i ̸= j. We have that gcd(Nj,n j) = 1. This is because gcd(nj,ni) = 1 for all i ̸= j as
they are pairwise prime . Since gcd(Nj,n j) = 1 we have that there exists an integer x j such that
Njxj ≡1
(mod nj),

A = a1N1x1 +...+aiNixi +...+akNkxk.

aiNixi ≡ai (mod ni). The second equivalence below follows

Example 2.3.4
When a bit sequence is transmitted a = (a1a2 ...an), a parity bit is computed and transmitted as well
where p ≡a1 +...+an (mod 2). The even parity bit e(a) os obtained by adding the bits of a and
returning the value of the sum modulo two. This sum is the number of ones in a; if it is an even
number e(a) is 0 else it is 1.
Example 2.3.5
A 10-digit ISBN (International Standard Book Number) code a = (a1 ...a10 where a10 is a check digit.
The check digits is (mod 1)1; an X represents a 10. The check digit computation involved is
10a1 +9a2 +8a3 +7a4 +6a5 +5a6 +4a7 +3a8 +2a9 +a10
(mod 11).

Proposition 2.7
If for m,m ∈Z, gcd(m,n) = 1 then
a ≡b
(mod mn) ⇔a ≡b
(mod m)

(mod n).
(2.8)
Proof. ⇒. If a ≡b (mod mn), the there exists an k such that a −b = kmn. Since m divides kmn then
m|a−b. Likewise, n|a−b. From the former we have a ≡b (mod m) and the latter a ≡b (mod n).
⇐. Let a ≡b (mod m)∧a ≡b (mod n). Then there exist M,N ∈Z such that a−b = Mm and a−b = Nn
respectively. Therefore Mm = Nn. Since n divides Nn it should divide Mm but because gcd(n,m) = 1
this implies n|M. Therefore M = ln for some integer l. Equation a−b = Mm = lnm = l(nm). Therefore
nm|a−b and we conclude a−b ≡0 (mod mn) or equivalently, a ≡b (mod mn).
The result is also a by product of the chinese remainder theorem.

2.6
Wilson’s theorem
Theorem 2.6
Wilson’s theorem onlyif
If p is a prime number, then show that
(p−1)! ≡−1
(mod p).
(2.12)
Proof.
Consider {1,2,... p−1}. No number x among them is divisible by p thus it is relatively prime to p and
thus it has an inverse (mod p).
It is not possible that x is its own inverse x−1 (mod p) unless x = 1 or x ≡p −1 ≡−1 (mod p) from
Theorem 2.12. Another way to say this is that if the inverse of x for some x such that 1 ≤x < p is x
the x2 ≡1 (mod p). Then p|(x−1)(x+1). Given that x < p this is only possible for x = 1 and x = −1
(mod p) i.e. x = p−1.
Thus for the remaining values x ∈{2,..., p−2}, we must have x ̸≡x−1 (mod p). Every pair cancels each
other i.e. x·x−1 ≡1 (mod p). Thus
2·3·4...(p−2) ≡1
(mod p)
Restoring the missing 1 and p−1 we have
1·2·3·4...(p−2)·(p−1) ≡p−1 ≡−1
(mod p)
Theorem 2.7
Wilson’s theorem
A natural number p is a prime number, if and only if
(p−1)! ≡−1
(mod p).
(2.13)
Proof.
⇐. If p is not prime then p = rs and r,s < p. The term (p −1)! includes all integers < p and thus r
and s. This implies that (p−1)! ≡0 (mod p). There is one exception and that is p = q2. For to have q
appearing in the product twice it would mean that q and 2q are part of the product, i.e. 2q ≤p−1. For
this to be the case we need p ≥4. Thus we verify by hand exhaustively that for p = 2,3 the Theorem is
true.
We can also claim that for p = q2 > 2q > q the (p −1)! ≡0 (mod p) is still the case. The latter is
equivalent to q2 > 2q i.e. q > 2. For q = 2 and thus p = q2 = 4 we have (p−1)! = 1·2·3 ≡2 (mod 4)
and thus (p−1)! ̸≡−1 (mod p).
⇒. If p is a prime number, then each 1,2,..., p −1 has an inverse, and among them, 1 and p −1 are
their own inverses.
x ≡x−1
(mod p) ⇒x·x ≡1
(mod p) ⇒x·x−1 ≡0
(mod p) ⇒(x−1)(x+1) ≡0
(mod p)
Thus p|(x −1)(x+1) and since 1 ≤x < p we have p|x−1 or p|x+1 or equivalently x−1 ≡0 (mod p)
or x+1 ≡0 (mod p) or equivalently x ≡1 (mod p) or x ≡−1 (mod p).
Thus all the other integers invert in pairs. Thus (p −1)! ≡(p −1) · (p −2) · ... · 2 · 1 ≡(p −1) · 1 ≡−1
(mod p).
A shorter proof follows.

Theorem 2.8
Wilson’s Theorem
A natural number p is a prime prime if and only if (p−1)! ≡−1 (mod p).
Proof. If p is not prime then p = rs and r,s < p. The term (p−1)! includes all integers < p and thus
r and s. This implies that (p −1)! ≡0 (mod p). There is one exception that p = q2. For to have q
appearing in the product twice it would mean that q and 2q are part of the product, ie 2q ≤p−1. For
this to be the case we need p ≥4. Thus we verify by hand exhaustively that for p = 2,3 the Thoerem is
true.
If p is a prime number, then 1,2,..., p −1 has a inverse, and 1 and p −1 are their own inverses.
Thus all the other integers inverse in pairs. Thus (p−1)! ≡(p−1)·(p−2)·...·2·1 ≡(p−1)·1 ≡−1
(mod p).
