2.7. FERMAT’S SEVERAL THEOREMS
69
2.7
Fermat’s several theorems
As of now we have established that for a prime number p, all integers a with 1 ≤a ≤p−1 are units that
is, the modular equations ax ≡1 (mod p) has a solution for x.
2.7.1
Fermat’s (little) theorem
Theorem 2.9
Fermat’s little theorem
Let p ∈N be a prime number, and let a ∈Z with gcd(a, p) = 1, then the following applies.
ap−1 ≡1
(mod p).
Proof. Consider a,2a,3a,...(p−1)a (mod p). Since p ∤a, all these values are non-zero and distinct mod
p. This is because if ia ≡ja (mod p), because p ∤a it should be p|i −j. This means p ≤|i −j|. But
both i, j are such that 0 ≤|i −j| ≤p −1 and thus p ≤p −1 which is impossible! The p −1 values
a,2a,...(p−1)a (mod p) can only be the only p−1 available
(mod p) i.e. 1,2,3,..., p−1 (possibly)
rearranged. Then taking their product one way or the other,
a·2a·...·(p−1)a ≡ap−1(p−1)! ≡1·2·...·(p−1) ≡(p−1)!
(mod p)
ap−1(p−1)! ≡(p−1)!
(mod p)
Since p is relatively prime to 1,2,3,...,(p−1) it is also to (p−1)!. Thus (ap−1 −1)(p−1)! ≡0 (mod p).
Thus it must be ap−1 −1 ≡0 (mod p) or equivalently ap−1 ≡1 (mod p) as needed.
The proof below is identical. Note that if p is a prime number, then gcd(a, p) = 1 or gcd(a, p) = p
for any a ∈Z. The latter is the case if a is a multiple of p. If a is not a multiple of p then p ∤a and the
only possibility is gcd(a, p) = 1.
Theorem 2.10
Fermat’s little theorem restated
Let p ∈N be a prime number, and let a ∈Z with p ∤aa, then the following applies.
ap−1 ≡1
(mod p).
Proof.
If p is a prime number, then gcd(i, p) = 1∨p. The latter is the case if i is a multiple of p. Say a is not
a multiple of p and gcd(a, p) = 1. Consider
(1a)p ·(2a)p ·...·((p−1)a)p,
where (ia)p! = (0)p and thus ia −0! = kp. This is so because otherwise ia = kp would imply that p
divides i or p divides a. The former is not possible for prime p since 0 < i < p, and the latter by way of
gcd(a, p) = 1. Furthermore (ia)p ̸= (ja)p for i ̸= j since otherwise (i−j)a ≡0 (mod k)p and we can use
the same consideration as before. Therefore
(1a)p,(2a)p,...,((p−1)a)p
is a permutation of 1,2,..., p−1 and we obtain the following.
(1a)p ·(2a)p ·...·((p−1)a)p = ((p−1)!ap−1)p = ((p−1)!)p,
or equivalently,
((p−1)!ap−1)p = ((p−1)!)p ⇔((p−1)!ap−1) ≡(p−1)! ⇔ap−1 ≡1
(mod p).
The last one is obtained given gcd(p,(p−1)!) = 1. Completed.

Example 2.7.2
a ≡−a (mod n) is equivalent to 2a ≡0 (mod n). If n = 2a this is trivially true. If n is odd, then

Theorem 2.12
If p is an odd prime (p ̸= 2) and p ∤a then the equation
x2 ≡a
(mod p)
has either exactly two distinct roots or no roots at all.
Proof. If there are roots to the modular equation the proof is complete. Otherwise let z be a solution i.e.
z2 ≡a (mod p). Since −z is such that (−z)2 ≡z2 ≡a (mod p), then −z is also a solution. Is it z ≡−z
(mod p)? This is so if p is an even number as it was shown prior to the statement of Theorem 2.12. It is
also possible that p is odd but then it must divide a. However the preconditions of the theorem disallow
the former (even number cannot be the case as p is an odd prime) and the latter (even number and p|a
is not possible, since p is odd and p ∤a).
Therefore there two distinct solutions z,−z (mod p) if one of them (say z) exists. Does there exist
a third (or fourth etc) solution? Let us call it w. Then w2 ≡z2 ≡a (mod p). This implies w2 −z2 ≡0
(mod p). Then (w−z)(w+z) ≡0 (mod p). Then p|w−z or p|w+z. In other words w ≡z or w ≡−z.
There are two and only two solutions then. No third or more!

2.7.3
Some interesting results
Corollary 2.12
Primality testing
Let p ∈Z+. Show that p is a prime number if and only is ap−1 ≡1 (mod p) for every a ̸= 0, and
a = 1,..., p−1. (We can augment a to be any integer a ∈Z+ such that p ∤a.)
Proof. If p is a prime number this follows from Fermat’s little theorem. For the other direction, if the
modular equation is true for all a, and p is not a prime number, let q be a divisor of p that is p = qk for
some integer k, and q > 1. Then ap−1 ≡1 (mod p), implies also qp−1 ≡1 (mod p), and since q|p we
also have qp−1 ≡1 (mod q). From the latter, q|qp−1 −1 we have q|1 or q ≤1 which contradicts q > 1.
Thus q does not exist and p is a prime number, as needed.

divides a−b. Thus a ≡b (mod p). Moreover aj(a−b) = aj+1 −ajb and thus aj+1 ≡ajb (mod p) and
also ap−1 ≡a jbp−1−j (mod p). Consider
ap −bp = (a−b)(ap−1 +ap−2b+...+bp−1).

Euler’s totient function φ(n) denotes the cardinality of Un that is φ(n) = |Un|, that is the number of
units mod n. Euler’s theorem is an extension of Fermat’s Little Theorem where the restriction of n being
a prime number has been relaxed.

## Page 83


Chapter 3
Primitive roots mod n
3.1
Order of a mod n
Z is the set of integers (positive, negative or zero). For a positive integer n > 0 the set Zn sometimes
denoted by Z/nZ or Z/n is the set of integers modulo n, thus representing the n equivalence classes that
the integers of Z can be split into depending on the remainder of their division by n. Zn is a cyclic group
under addition, and a commutative ring under multiplication and addition. The ring is a field for a prime
n.
From Euler’s formula for a ∈Z and gcd(a,n) = 1, we have that aφ(n) ≡1 (mod n). Is it possible that
ak ≡1 (mod n) for smaller k < φ(n)? We have already mentioned that (−1)2 ≡(n−1)2 ≡1 (mod n).
Definition 3.1
Order modn
For n ∈N and n > 1, and a ∈Zn with gcd(a,n) = 1, we define the order ordn(a) to be the smallest
positive integer k such that ak ≡1 (mod n).
By Euler’s theorem aφ(n) ≡1 (mod n) implies that k|φ(n). (If this was not the case φ(n) = kq + r,
0 ≤r < k would imply ar ≡1 (mod n) for an r < k, an impossibility given that ordn(a) = k.) An element
a as defined above that has order φ(n) is called a primitive root mod n.
Theorem 3.1
Let k = ordn(a) be as defined earlier. For all m we have am ≡1 (mod n) if and only if k|m.
Proof.
⇒.
If k|m we have m = ks for some integer s. Then
am ≡(ak)s ≡1
(mod n).
⇐.
For the other way if am ≡1 (mod n), Let m = kq+r, where 0 ≤r < k. If r = 0 we are done since then
k|m. Consider that r ̸= 0. Since ak ≡1 (mod n) and thus (ak)q ≡1 (mod n) and also am ≡1 (mod n),
we have
1 ≡am ≡akq+r ≡(ak)q ·ar ≡ar
(mod n).
For ar to be ≡1 (mod n) given that 0 < r < k is impossible since k is the smallest index for which this
is true. The only possibility is that r = 0 that was put aside and the result follows by accepting the
dismissed case r = 0 as the only possibility.

Let k = ordn(a) be as defined earlier. Then aφ(n) ≡1 (mod n) implies that k|φ(n).
Corollary 3.2
Let k = ordp(a), a ∈Z, where p is a prime number. Then aφ(p) ≡1 (mod p) implies that k|p−1.
Proposition 3.1
If k = ordn(a) be as defined earlier, then am has order k if and only if gcd(m,k) = 1.
Proof. Let d = gcd(m,k) and let q = ordn(am).
We need to show that q = k if and only if d = 1.
⇒.
Let q = k. We will show that d = 1. Let d > 1. Then k = dx and m = dy, for some integer x,y. Note
that x < k and y < m. Then we have
(am)x ≡amx ≡adyx ≡(ak)y ≡1
(mod n)
Therefore the order q of am is x, where x < k, is smaller than the order q = k of am. This is impossible by
the minimality of q = k, q = ordn(am). Thus it should be x = k = q which implies d = 1. Result shown.
⇐.
If d = 1 = gcd(m,k) we show that for q = ordn(am) we have q = k. Since q = ordn(am) we have
amq ≡(am)q ≡1
(mod n)
This means that k|mq from Theorem 2.2. Since d = gcd(k,m) = 1, we have k|q. Thus k ≤q. We also
have
(am)k ≡(ak)m ≡1m ≡
(mod n)
Thus q|k i.e. q ≤k. From k ≤q and q ≤k the result k = q follows.
Corollary 3.3
Let n ∈N with n > 1 and let a,b ∈Z with gcd(a,n) = 1 and gcd(b,n) = 1, For ordn(a) = k and ordn(b) = l
if gcd(k,l) = 1 then ordn(ab) = kl.
Proof. Let ordn(ab) = m. Then
(ab)kl ≡(ak)l(bl)k ≡1
(mod n)
Therefore m|kl. Moreover
1 ≡((ab)m)k = (ak)m(bkm) ≡bkm
(mod n)
This means l|km. Since gcd(k,l) = 1 we have l|m. Likewise, if we consider ((ab)m)l instead, we conclude
k|m instead. Since gcd(k,l) = 1 we have kl|m. This implies kl ≤m. But since m is the order of ab we
must also have, by the first derivation above, kl ≥m. Thus kl = m.
Lemma 3.1
For n ∈N, and a ∈Z such that gcd(a,n) = 1. Let ordn(a) = k, Then for all b,c ∈N and a the following

3.2
Primitive roots
A unit g mod n is a g ∈Zn such that gcd(g,n) = 1. By Euler’s theorem gφ(n) ≡1 (mod n).
A unit g mod n is a primitive root if its order is φ(n).
Definition 3.2
Primitive root mod n
Let n ∈N with n > 1. An integer g ∈Z is a primitive root modulo n if g mod n is a unit and thus
gcd(g,n) = 1 and by extension g mod n ∈Un and has order φ(n).
A unit g (mod n) is a primitive root if it generates all Un. For this reason g is called a generator of
Un. That is Un = {1,g,g2,...,gφ(n)−1}. This implies that ordn(g) = φ(n). Moreover Un is cyclic and g is
its generator.
Example 3.2.1
(Note that 32 implies a 32 (mod 7).) For n = 7 we have
Z7 −{0} = U7 = {1,31,32,33,34,35} = {1,3,2,6,4,5}.
Thus g = 3 is a primitive root.
Corollary 3.4
If Un has a primitive root g, then all the primitive roots of Un are those gq such that gcd(q,φ(n)) = 1.
In particular, there are φ(φ(n)) primitive roots modn.
Proof. If g is a primitive root the ordn(g) = φ(n). By Proposition 3.1 ordn(gk) = φ(n) if and only if
gcd(k,φ(n)) = 1. The number of values k such that this is true is φ(φ(n)). All elements of Un are of the
form gi and will thus be found this way.
Example 3.2.2
If g is a primitive root of Un then gm has order φ(n) if and only if gcd(m,φ(n)) = 1.
Solution. This is a consequence of Proposition 3.1 for ordn(g) = φ = k there.

Polynomials
Proposition 3.5
Let (S,+,·) be a field and let f ∈S[x], where f ̸= 0 be a polynomial over S. Then f has at most deg(f)
roots.
Proof. A proof follows by induction.
Base case. Let n = deg(f) = 0 be the degree of f, that is, f(x) = a0. Since f(x) ̸= 0 we have a0 ̸= 0
and thus the polynomial has at most 0 roots that is 0 roots obviously. The base case has been proven.
Inductive step. Assume that the claim holds for polynomials of degree up to n −1. Consider n =
deg(f) ≥1. If polynomial f has at most n roots, then we are done. Consider f(x) as follows.
f(x) = anxn +...+a1x+a0 ∈S[x],
with f(x) being a polynomial of degree n. If f(x) has no roots in S, then we are also done. Otherwise
assume that f(x) has at least one root, and let it be r that is f(r) = 0 (mod n). Then we have the
following.
f(x) = f(x)−f(a) = an(xn −rn)+...+a1(x−r) =
n
∑
i=1
ai(xi −ri).
We have that xi −ri = (x−r)∑i−1
j=0 x jri−1−j. Then
f(x) = (x−r)g(x),
where deg(g) ≤n−1. Consider another root of f and let it be s. For s ̸= r, we have 0 = f(s) = (s−r)g(s).
Since s ̸= r this implies g(s) = 0. This is because element s−r ̸= 0 has an inverse and multiplying both
sides of 0 = (s −r)g(s) with that inverse we conclude that g(s) = 0 that is s is a root of g. By the
induction hypothesis applied to g there can be no more than n−1 roots of g such as s. Adding to this
root r we can have at most n roots for f.
Consider Zp = Z/pZ, where p is a prime number. Then (Zp,+,·) is a field. Thus the following result
due to Lagrange is applicable. See the next problem, already proven for the more general case of a field
in this problem.
3.3.1
Lagrange’s theorem

3.3. POLYNOMIALS
83
Theorem 3.2
Lagrange’s theorem
Consider a polynomial f(x) of degree n with integer coefficients. Let p be a prime number.
f(x) = anxn +an−1xn−1 +...+a1x+a0.
Consider an mod p ̸= 0. That is we exclude from consideration the case that all coefficients are multiples
of p or equivalently exclude all cases p|ai for all i = 0,1,...,n. Then, f(x) can have at most n distinct
zeroes mod p.
In other words, f(x) ≡0 (mod p), has at most deg(f) = n roots mod p, unless all (presumed integer)
coefficients of f are divisible by p. No more than n elements of Up can thus be a root of f(x) ≡0
(mod p). The “expression n zeroes mod p” is to be read “n congruence classes of solutions (zeroes) mod
p”. Thus if k, an elements of Up is a root of f(x) ≡0 (mod p) and f(x) ≡0 (mod p) so do we expect
for k + p,k +2p,....
Proof. A proof follows by induction.
Base case. For n = deg(f) = 0 we have f(x) = a0 that has 0 roots mod p for f(x) ≡a0 ≡0 (mod p),
for p ∤a0. (Or for n = 0, an mod p ̸= 0.)
Inductive step. Consider n = deg(f) ≥1. If f has at most n roots we are done. By the inductive step
f has at least one root and let it be r. Consider the polynomials
f(x) = g(x)(x−r)+R(x),
with deg(R) < deg(x−r) = 1 i.e. deg(R) = 0. We can write R(x) = c for some integer c. Set x = r and
we have
(f(r) ≡0
(mod p)) ≡g(r)·0+c
(mod p)
Then
c ≡0
(mod p).
This means R(x) = R = c = 0 (mod p) and therefore
f(x) ≡g(x)(x−r)
(mod p).
Then, deg(x −r) = 1 and deg(g) ≤n −1. By the inductive hypothesis the former has one root (and it
is r) and the latter has at most n −1 roots mod p. Combined f(x) has at most n roots mod p, as
needed.
Proposition 3.6
If p is a prime number and d is a divisor of p−1, then the polynomial f(x) = xd −1 ∈Zp[x] has exactly
d roots.
Proof. Since d|p −1 then p −1 = dk. Consider xp−1 −1. By Euler’s (or FLT) all non zero elements
1,..., p−1 are roots of it. Therefore it has p−1 different roots. Moreover
(xd)k −1 = (xd −1)((xd)k−1 +...+(xd)0) = f(x)g(x),
where g(x) is a polynomial of degree dk −d = p−1−d. By the previous result f has at most d roots,
and g at most dk −d = p −1 −d. But the product has at least p −1 roots all of them distinct. Thus
f(x) has exactly d and g(x) p−1−d roots. The result follows.
Consider the polynomial f(x) = xp−1 −1, where p is a prime number. By Fermat’s Little theorem we
know. that
f(x) ≡0
(mod p)
⇔
xp−1 ≡1
(mod p),
for any x such that gcd(x, p) = 1. There are p−1 such x and these are the elements of Up = {1,2,..., p−
1} since |Up| = φ(p) = p−1. Lagrange’s theorem confirms that there are at most p−1 distinct solutions
(roots) for f(x) ≡0 (mod p).

Proposition 3.10
Conditions for primitive roots mod n and 2n
Let n be equal to two (n = 2) or to an odd prime integer power n = pa, a ≥1. Then there exists a
primitive root mod n if and only if there exists a primitive root mod 2n.

gk ≡1
(mod 2n).

Corollary 3.6
No primitive roots mod 8
There are no primitive roots mod n, where n = 8.
Proof. For n = 8, we have |U8| = 4 and contains 1,3,5,7. For all these value x = 1,3,5,7 we have x2 ≡1
(mod 8). For an x to be a primitive root we require x4 ≡1 (mod 8) i.e. the minimal power/order of x to
be 4 and not 2, since φ(8) = 4.

Theorem 3.6
Primitive roots mod pa, a ≥1
There are primitive roots mod pa for all a ≥1, where p is an odd prime number.
Proof. From Proposition 3.12, if g is a primitive root mod p, then either g or g + p is a primitive root
mod pa for all a ≥1.
By Proposition 3.9, there exists a primitive root g mod p.
Thus there exist
primitive roots mod pa. Furthermore if there exists one primitive root mod p, from Eq. (3.4) there are
φ(φ(p)) = φ(p−1) such primitive roots mod p and by extension mod pa. Every primitive root mod p is
a generator of Up and thus Up is cyclic.
Theorem 3.7
Primitive roots mod 2pa, a ≥1
There are primitive roots mod 2pa for all a ≥1, where p is an odd prime number.
Proof. Existentially, this follows by way of Corollary 3.6 and Proposition 3.10 and Proposition 3.4. This
is going to be proved indirectly. By the previous Proposition 3.6 we know that there is a primitive root,
call it g, mod pa, a ≥1.
We are going to show that if g is odd then g is also a primitive root mod 2pa. Otherwise, g+ pa is odd,
and in this case we can show that g+ pa is a primitive root mod 2pa.
Case 1: g is odd. Then, the sequence,
g,g2,g3,...gφ(pa),
is a sequence of odd integer and are distinct mod pa. Then they are also distinct mod 2pa as well. Note
that
φ(2pa) = φ(2)φ(pa) = φ(p2).
Thus the sequence above enumerates all unit not only of Upa but also U2pa since
|Upa| = |U2pa| = φ(pa).
Case 2 g is even. The g+ pa is odd and proceed likewise.
This concludes the theorem.
The conclusion leads to the following summarizing theorem.

residue i.e. it is a quadratic non-residue. The set of units (Z/nZ)x for prime n will be represented by

Example 3.6.1
Let p be an odd prime. Show the following: If a is a quadratic residue mod p and b is a quadratic
residue mod p then ab is a quadratic residue mod p as well.
Proof. If a is a q.r. mod p then there exists x such that x2 ≡a (mod p). If b is a q.r. mod p then
there exists y such that y2 ≡b (mod p). Then obviously x2y2 = (xy)2 ≡ab (mod p), implying that ab is
a quadratic residue mod p as well.

3.9
Quadratic residues
Definition 3.5
Quadratic residue
For p ∈N, where p is an odd prime number, and for any a ∈Zp we say that a is a quadratic residue
(mod p) if and only if the congruence
x2 ≡a
(mod p)
has a solution for 0 < x < p.
The definition extends to the general case of an integer n rather than of odd prime p.
Definition 3.6
Quadratic residue
For n ∈N, and for any a ∈Zn we say that a is a quadratic residue (mod n) if and only if the congruence
x2 ≡a
(mod n)
has a solution for 0 < x < n.
Let a ≡b (mod p), for p an odd prime number, then x2 ≡b (mod p) has a solution if and only if
x2 ≡a (mod p) does also. Quadratic residuocity (with respect to odd prime number p) is relevant to
the introduction of the Legendre symbol. Later we extend it to the introduction of the Jacobi symbol.
Example 3.9.1
For Z7 find the q.r (quadratic residues) and the q.nr. (quadratic non-residues) mod 7.
Solution. 12,22,32,42,52,62 (mod 7) are respectively 1,4,2,2,4,1 (mod 7). Thus 1,2,4 are the quadratic
residues, and 3,5,6 are the quadratic non-residues and 0 sometimes is counted, sometimes not!

Theorem 3.12
If p is an odd prime then (p−1)/2 of the units (mod p) are quadratic residues, (p−1)/2 are quadratic
non-residues and there is nothing left unaccounted for.
Proof. Consider ±1,±2,...,±(p−1)/2 and take the square of those elements. These elements account
for all the units
(mod p). If b2 ≡a (mod p), then (−b)2 ≡a (mod p) as well. The (p−1)/2 distinct
values (of the squares) are the quadratic residues. Everything else is a quadratic non-residue or 0.

3.11
Pythagorean triplets
Let a2 +b2 = c2 be the pythagorean identity with a ≤b ≤c. A triplet (a,b,c) is a solution to the identity.
It is called primitive if gcd(a,b) = 1. If (a,b,c) is a triplet so is (ma,mb,mc) for every integer m.
One way to generate triplets is to use the identity (x+y)2 = x2 +2xy+y2 and (x−y)2 = x2 −2xy+y2
which implye (x+y)2 −(x−y)2 = 4xy. Set x = X2 and y = Y 2 we have
Thus
Theorem 3.17

pythagorean triplet, since
(X2 +Y 2)2 = (2XY)2 +(X2 −Y 2)2
Moreover Z(a,b,c) are other triplets, for X,Y,Z ∈Z.
Theorem 3.18
If p is a prime factor of n with p ≡3 (mod 4) and p divides n an odd number of times. Then n cannot
be expressed as a sum of squares.

A linear congruential generator (LCG) is one such that xi+1 ≡axi +b (mod n), where gcd(a,n) = 1.
If gcd(a−1,n) = 1 the x0 should be chosen so that gcd(x0 −b(1−a)−1,n) = 1
The period of LCG is its modulus n if and only if gcd(b,n) = 1, a ≡1 (mod p) for every prime p such
that p|n, and a ≡1 (mod 4) if 4|n.
A Blum-Blum-Shub (BBS) sequence is one where n = pq and p,q are primes such that p ≡q ≡3
(mod p). A seed x0 is chosen so that gcd(x0,n) = 1. Then x2
i+1 ≡x2
i (mod n). The output is xi (mod 2).
For a long period gcd(φ(p−1),φ(q−1)) is small compared to n.
3.12
Public Key Cryptography
A very brief overview of applications of number theory is given in this section
3.12.1
Diffie-Hellman key exchange
It uses Up exponentiation.
Choose a large prime p, and an element g ∈Up, where g is preferably
a primitive root.
This information is public.
The following two pieces of information are secret for
each party involved. Alice chooses a secret exponent 1 ≤a ≤p −1. Bod chooses a secret exponent
1 ≤b ≤p −1. Alice publishes ga (mod p) and Bob gb
(mod p). The other party picks the other’s
published info and compute gagb (mod p). Only they know both multiplicands and thus their product.
The only way to retrieve from ga,gb,g, p the a or b is by a slow discrete logarithm process.


## Page 131


3.12. PUBLIC KEY CRYPTOGRAPHY
123
3.12.2
RSA
Let p,q are two large primes p ̸= q. Let n = pq and choose an e such that
gcd(e,φ(n)) = gcd(e,(p−1)(q−1)) = 1
1. Public key. It is the pair (e,n). The modular equation
ed ≡1
(mod (p−1)(q−1))
is then solved. Because gcd(e,(p−1)(q−1)) = 1, there exists one and only one solution modφ(n).
2. Private key. It is the pair (p,q) or for convenience the triplet (d, p,q) (d is not needed as it can be
recomputed from the public key and private p,q).
The following information is public or private for a a party, say Alice. (Similarly for the other part,
Bob.)
Alice’s Public Information: (eA,nA)
Alices’ Private Information: (dA, pA,qA).
Communication from Alice to Bob: sending encrypted message M. In order for Alice to send Bob
a message M, Alice must retrieve Bob’s Public information (eB,nB). Then for message M Alice computes
C ≡MeB (mod n)B. It transmits C to Bob, not M.
Communication received by Bob: recovery of M Bob received C from Alice. He retrieves his private
information (dB, pB,qB). He then performs the following computation (note nB = pBqB).
CdB ≡(MeB)dB ≡MeBdB ≡M
(mod nB)
The correctness of the decruption process B follows by way of Euler’s theorem. Bob chose nB = pBqB
where
eBdB ≡1
(mod (pB −1)(qB −1)) ⇔eBdB ≡1
(mod φ(nB))
Therefore there exists a k ∈Z such that
eBdB −1 = kφ(nB).
Then from the decryption process, we have
MeBdB ≡MeBdB−1 ·M ≡Mkφ(nB) ·M ≡(Mφ(nB))k ·M ≡(1)k ·M ≡M
(mod nB),
where we used Euler’s theorem for
Mφ(nB) ≡1
(mod nB).
RSA’s difficulty relies on the perceived difficulty of factoring n into p,q and thus computing d.
Equivalently on computing d from e,n alone without factoring n.
For RSA message M must be close to the size of φ(n). Thus padding may need to be performed if
M is small (or an attacker may rely on brute force techniques). Because of these, RSA is primarily being
used to transmit secret keys, and other methods are used for transmitting messages such as M.

Chapter 4
Primality testing
4.1
Carmichael numbers
Definition 4.1
Carmichael numbers
A composite natural number n > 1, n ∈N, is a Carmichael number if it satisfies the following.
an ≡a
(mod n),
∀a ∈Z.
Definition 4.2
Carmichael numbers; an alternative definition
A composite natural number n > 1, n ∈N, is a Carmichael number if it satisfies the following.
an−1 ≡1
(mod n),
∀a ∈Z : gcd(a,n) = 1.
Example 4.1.1
Integer n = 561 is a Carmichael number. Show that n = 561 is not a prime number by finding an a
such that
an−1 ̸≡1
(mod n),
that’s. a violation of Fermat’s little theorem is established. How much is
an−1
(mod n),
then?
Solution.
Consider n = 561. It is obviously a composite number as n is divisible by 3. Thus n is not a prime number.
For a prime number n, an−1 ≡1 (mod n) for all a = 1,2,...,n−1, by way of Fermat’s little theorem.
Consider a = 2. It can be shown that 2560 ≡1 (mod 561). Obviously then 2561 ≡2 (mod 561).
Consider now a = 3. 3560 ≡375 (mod 561) ̸≡1 (mod 561), and a violation of Fermat’s little Theorem
is obtained for the premise n = 561 is a prime number. Note however that 3561 ≡3 (mod 561). Even
though 561 is a Carmichael number and for every a with gcd(a,561) = 1, we have a560 ≡1 (mod 561),
note that a = 3 has gcd(3,561) = 3 ̸= 1.
4.1.1
Korselt’s theorem
It is also known as Korselt’s criterion. It characterizes Carmichael numbers. A positive (composite)
integer n is a Carmichael number if and only if it is square free and for all prime divisors p of n we have
125


## Page 134


126
CHAPTER 4. PRIMALITY TESTING
p−1|n−1. One can directly and obviously use the Korselt theorem to show that all Carmichael numbers
are odd.
The proof is deferred after the proof of two technical lemmas.
Lemma 4.1
All Carmichael numbers are odd.
Proof.
(Using Korselt’s theorem.)
Say n is a Carmichael number and it is even. Then n = 2m, where m is odd since a Carmichael number
is square-free and thus m can not be even. Then m has at least one prime factor, let it that be p, and
it must be odd (including other ones). We thus have p|m ⇒p|n. Then by Korselt’s theorem p−1|n−1
would imply that an even number p−1 divides an odd number n−1, a contradiction.
(Another proof without using Korselt’s theorem.)
Say n is even. Consider a = (−1) and by Carmichael numbers definition we have the following.
(−1)n ≡(−1)
(mod n) ⇒1 ≡(−1)
(mod n) ⇒2 ≡0
(mod n)
This implies n|2. Thus n ≤2. For a positive n this means n = 1 or n = 2. A Carmichael number is n > 1.
Thus the only case left is n = 2. But a Carmichael number is composite and two is prime. Thus n cannot
be even, and must be odd.
Lemma 4.2
Let n = p1p2, p1 ̸= p2, where p1, p2 ∈Z+ are prime greater than 2. Let a ∈Z such that
an−1 ≡1
(mod p1)
and
an−1 ≡1
(mod p2).
Then, the following applies.
an−1 ≡1
(mod p1p2).
The result can be generalized by using induction on integer n having more than two prime factors.
Proof.
If a is as given then there exist K,L such that
an−1 −1 = Kp1,
an−1 −1 = Lp2.
Since p1, p2 are prime different from each other gcd(p1, p2) = 1, and therefore, there exists x,y integer
such that
xp1 +yp2 = 1.
Multiply both sides by an−1 −1. We derive the following.
(an−1 −1)(xp1 +yp2) = an−1 −1 ⇒xLp1p2 +yKp1p2 = an−1 −1,
from which we conclude an−1 ≡1 (mod p1p2).
Theorem 4.1
Korselt
A positive (composite) integer n is a Carmichael number if and only if it is square free and for all prime
divisors p of n we have p−1|n−1.

Lemma 4.3
Up is a group under multiplication mod p.
Proof.
It suffices to show that Up is closed under under multiplication mod p. Consider a,b ∈Up. For prime p
and 1 ≤a,b < p we have gcd(a, p) = gcd(b, p) = 1 and thus a−1,b−1 exist. Then
(ab) ∈Up,

All other properties (e.g. associativity) hold for Up by way of holding for Zp.

4.3.1
Probabilistic Turing machines
Definition 4.3
Probabilistic Turing machine
A probabilistic Turing machine is a non deterministic polynomial time Turing machine T such that
for each configuration of T there are at most two possible follow-up configurations (non-deterministic
steps), and T chooses which one of the two to pursue (take) by flipping a coin.
Thus the paths of different configurations resembles a binary tree. For k coin flips and paths of length k
a given path is pursued with probability equal 1/2k.
4.3.2
Class BPP
Definition 4.4
Class BPP
Let 0 ≤ε < 1/2. A (probabilistic polynomial time) Turing Machine M decides language L with error
probability ε if
• for a w ∈L we have Pr[M accepts w ] ≥1−ε,
• for a w ̸∈L we have Pr[M rejects w ] ≥1−ε, or equivalently Pr[M accept w ] ≤ε.
BPP is the class of languages that are decided by a probabilistic polynomial time Turing machine with
error probability ε, as defined.

4.4. FERMAT PRIMALITY TESTING
135
4.4
Fermat primality testing
4.4.1
A Fermat little theorem-based compositeness test
Theorem 4.4
Algorithm 5 is a probabilistic algorithm for testing whether n is composite.
Proof.
We note that in Algorithm 5 we check for the compositeness of n for a uniformly at random drawn a
in the range 2,3,...,n −2 in two ways: (a) we first perform the trivial check of whether gcd(a,n) > 1,
and (b) then check whether an−1 ̸≡1 (mod n). If any of the two checks succeeds we not only conclude
that n is composite but a serves as a witness (proof) of the compositeness of n. The latter (b) is a
consequence of Fermat’s little theorem. If n is a prime number, then any a picked through line 1 is such
that gcd(a,n) = 1. Therefore by Fermat’s little theorem we expect an−1 ≡1 (mod n). If the control
expression of line 2 evaluates to true i.e. there is a common divisor other than one between a, which is
2 ≤a < n−1 and n indeed n is a composite number. If the control expression of line 4 evaluates to true
i.e. an−1 ̸≡1 (mod n) this also guarantees by Fermat’s little theorem that the answer Composite is the
correct answer. However, an answer through line 5 does not guarantee that n is prime: we checked only
for one a in Algorithm 5 and not for all a. We prefer to call this algorithm FermatCompositenessTest for
the following reason: when the algorithm declares n as Composite the answer is always correct. Otherwise
it declares n as PseudoPrime. This means n can be Prime or Composite. One test for one value of a is
not enough to determine reliably the primeness of a. Note that neither 1 nor n−1 are picked as choices of
a. For both such cases the answer for an−1 is equivalent to 1 mod n and provides no useful information.
Algorithm 5: FermatCompositenessTest(n)
Input: n > 4 is odd; a is in {2,3,...,n−2}
Output: n is composite or pseudoprime
1 Pick a ∈{2,3,...,n−2} uniformly at random ;
2 if gcd(a,n) ̸= 1 then
3
return Composite
4 end
5 if an−1 ̸≡1 (mod n) then
/* Compositeness check */
6
return Composite ;
/* a is a witness of n’s compositeness */
7 else
8
return PseudoPrime ;
/* n is either prime or composite */
9 end
We strengthen the capability and reliability of Algorithm 5 by introducing Algorithm 6 a Fermat little
theorem based primality testing algorithm.


## Page 144


136
CHAPTER 4. PRIMALITY TESTING
4.4.2
A Fermat little theorem-based primality test
Theorem 4.5
Algorithm 6 is a probabilistic algorithm for testing whether n is composite or prime.
Proof. Algorithm 6 is in fact t runs of Algorithm 5; the t runs are independent of each other and each
run picks potentially (but not necessarily) a different value for a. Whereas the previous algorithm picks
one a this one picks t a’s from {2,3,...,n−2} uniformly at random.
Algorithm 6: FermatPrimalityTest
Input: n > 4 is odd; number of runs is t
Output: n is composite or pseudoprime
1 i = 0 ;
2 do
3
i = i+1 ;
4
Pick a ∈{2,3,...,n−2} uniformly at random ;
5
if gcd(a,n) ̸= 1 then
6
return Composite
7
end
8
if an−1 ̸≡1 (mod n) then
/* Compositeness check */
9
return Composite ;
/* a is a witness of n’s compositeness */
10
end
11 while i < t;
12 return PseudoPrime ;
/* n is either prime or composite */
If n is a prime, then any 1 ≤a < n has gcd(a,n) = 1. Therefore by Fermat’s tittle theorem we have
an−1 ≡1 (mod n). If an a can be found such that an−1 ̸≡1 (mod n), then the a becomes a witness of
the compositeness of n. We shall denote it as Fw(a,n) or say a ∈Fw(n) to denote that a is one of Fermat
witnesses of the compositeness of n. Line 6 of Algorithm 5 or Line 9 of Algorithm 6 report correctly
this, if such an a has been picked. Moreover, LIne 3 of Algorithm 5 or Line 6 of Algorithm 6 also report
the case where a is such that gcd(a,n) ̸= 1, an obvious proof of the compositeness of n. When either
algorithm reports PseudoPrime this means one of two things: (a) that n is indeed a prime number or (b)
the test is not comprehensive enough to determine that n is indeed a composite number. Sometimes we
call such an a for which an−1 ≡1 (mod n) a Fermat non-witness of the compositeness of n. We shall
denote it with Fnw(a,n) or say a ∈Fnw(n). Therefore we have the following.
a ∈Fw(n) : an−1 ̸≡1
(mod n),gcd(a,n) = 1,
and
a ∈Fnw(n) : an−1 ≡1
(mod n),gcd(a,n) = 1
For a Carmichael number n, which is a composite number, we have the following from a prior discussion.
If we denote with Carmichael(n) the set of a that satisfy the Carmichael condition an−1 ≡1 (mod n) for
a such that gcd(a,n) = 1 we conclude that the Carmichael(n) set contains alll those integers.
∀a ∈Z,a ∈Carmichael(n) : an−1 ≡1
(mod n),gcd(a,n) = 1.
Thus for a composite Carmichael number n every a such that gcd(a,n) = 1 is a Fermat non-witness
Fnw(a,n). A prime number n will be reported through line 12 as PseudoPrime. A composite number n
could be reported as composite immediately through line 6 or through FermatLittleTheorem testing in
Line 9 for a properly chosen a. But it is possible that the values a used would never lead to determining
that that n were a Composite number through line 6. Furthermore the choice of a given a (or all a)

Algorithm 6 is a probabilistic algorithm for testing whether n is prime or composite.
Proposition 4.1
Probabibility of successful reporting
The probability that the output of Algorithm 6 is Composite given that n is a composite number but
not a Carmichael number is at least 1−2−t.
Proof.
In other words, if n is a prime number Algorithm 6 returns Pseudoprime with probability one. If n is a
composite number but not a Carmichael number then Algorithm 6 return composite with probability at
least 1−2−t. Suppose that ∃a ∈Un such that

We have |Bn|+|Cn| = φ(n) = |Un|. For a composite n, we have |Dn| > 1 as it contains the prime factors
of n, minimally. Note that a ∈Un implies gcd(a,n) = 1. Set Cn is a subgroup. Consider k,l ∈Cn with
kn−1 ≡1 (mod n) and ln−1 ≡1 (mod n). We conclude easily that (kl)n−1 ≡1 (mod n) to prove closure.
Sidelining the claims about the obvious properties, consider K = k−1 the multiplicative inverse of k and

(kl)−1 ∈Cn. Thus Cn is a subgroup of Un and the order of Cn divides the order of Un which is φ(n).
Thus if Cn ̸= Un (e.g. n is not a Carmichael number), then |Cn| ≤|Un|/2 and |Bn| ≥|Un|/2.
Lemma 4.5
Running time of algorithm 6
The running time of Algorithm 6 is O(t ·lgn·M(n)), where M(n) is the cost of multiplying n-bit integers.
Proof. Exponentiation involves O(lgn) multiplication. Depending on how we implement integer multipli-
cation the overall time complexity is O(t ·lgn·M(n)), where M(n) is the computational cost (bit model)
of multiplying n-bit integers.
Corollary 4.5
FermatPrimalityTest ∈BPP
Algorithm 6 belongs to class BPP, known as Bounded-error Probabilistic Polynomial time class.
Proof. We shall map (call) L and w to Prime and n respectively. If n is a prime number, it is decided by
Algorithm 6 that it is a Prime (and output PseudoPrime is printed) with probability 1.
If n is a composite number, it is decided by Algorithm 6 that it is a Composite number (and output
Composite is printed) and thus rejected being a Prime/PseudoPrime with probability at least 1−1/2t by
way of Proposition 4.1.
In other words, n can be wrongly decided by Algorithm 6 that it is a Prime (and output PseudoPrime is
printed) with probability at most 1/2t.

Algorithm 8 performs t times the tasks of Algorithm 7 which is a simpler probabilistic algorithm. Al-
gorithm 7 checks Euler’s theorem for a single random value a.
Algorithm 8 checks Euler’s theorem
for a t uniformly at random selected values a. The former is weak algorithm and for this we call it a
compositeness test algorithm. The latter is a more reliable one and is referred to as a primality test
algorithm.
Theorem 4.6
Algorithm 8 is a probabilistic algorithm for testing whether n is composite or not.
Proof.
The algorithm tests for two conditions a number of times. We denote as t the numer of times these
conditions are tested. Every time these conditions are tested we pick a random a from the set {2,3,...,n−
2} of cardinality n −1. The first condition C1(a,n) checks whether a has a common divisor with n. A
positive answer serves makes a a witness of the compositeness of n. We call then a an Euler (test)
compositeness witness and denote it by Ew(a,n) or the set of witnesses for n is denoted as Ew(n).
C1(a,n) : gcd(a,n) > 1.
The second condition is Euler’s criterion.
C2(a,n) :

(mod n).
Therefore a becomes a Euler test compositeness witness if and only if the following applies.
a ∈Ew(n) ⇔C1(a,n)∨C2(a,n).
By way of (C1(a,n)∨C2(a,n)) = C1(a,n) ∧C2(a,n) we obtain the following condition for Euler (test)
compositeness non-witnesses Enw(a,n) or the set Enw(n).
a ∈Enw(n) ⇔C1(a,n)∧C2(a,n).
The proof of correctness of both algorithms follows from the prior discussion and thus omitted.

4.6
Riemann hypothesis associated primality tests
4.6.1
Perfect powers
One of the first steps in Miller’s algorithm it is to detect whether an integer n is a perfect power or not.
We say that natural number n is a perfect power if there exist positive integers x,m such that n = xm.
Definition 4.5
Perfect power
Given a natural integer number n we say n is a perfect power if there exist a natural integer number x
and a natural integer number m > 1 such that
n = xm
Lemma 4.7
Perfect power algorithm
One can use binary search and thus time polylogarithmic in n to determine x,m. This is NaivePerfect-
Power(n).
Proof.
Since n = xm ≥2m we observe that m ≤lgn or m ≤⌊lgn⌋. Thus the integer candidate values for m are
2,3,...,⌊lgn⌋. If A = lgn the number of values m we need to search for is at most A−1.
Algorithm 9: NaivePerfectPower(n) : Determine if n is a perfect power
Input: n greater than 2
Output: x,m such that xm = n or NotaPPower
1 A = lgn ;
/* Bound for m is A */
2 for i = 2,3,...,⌊A⌋do
3
m = i ;
4
x = BinarySearch([1,y],m) ; /* Determine 1 < x ≤n such that xm < n, (x+1)m ≥n */
5
if xm == n then
6
return (x,m) ;
/* Perfect Power */
7
end
8

9

## Page 154

CHAPTER 4. PRIMALITY TESTING
4.6.2
Miller primality test under GRH : Miller1
Miller ([6]) describes two deterministic algorithms that work correctly under the Generalized Riemann
Hypothesis (GRH). Miller cites the Extended Riemann Hypothesis for a Dirichlet function; the Generalized
Riemann Hypothesis for Dirichlet L-functions is equivalent to the Extended Riemann hypothesis for the
problem in hand. The first algorithm, that will be referred to as Miller1, appears on page 303 of [6].
The second algorithm, that will be referred to as Miller2, appears on page 308 of [6]. Miller1 is the
deterministic algorithm turned by Rabin [8] into a probabilistic algorithm, and analyzed in [8]. This
probabilistic algorithm will be referred to as Rabin-Miller. Monier [7] proved that the conditions used in
Miller1 and Miller2 are equivalent. The probabilistic version of Miller2 will be referred to as the Miller-
Rabin algorithm. Because of the equivalence of the conditions of Miller1 and Miller2, the Rabin analysis
originally for the probabilistic algorithm of the modified Miller1 applies also to the modified Miller2.
Miller1 and also Algorithm 11 known as Miller2, if the GRH is incorrect, might generate an incorrect
answer. This uncertainty is not present in Miller-Rabin or Rabin-Miller.
Theorem 4.7
Miller [6], pages 303-304
Algorithm 10, Miller1, is a deterministic algorithm for testing whether n is prime or composite, under
the GRH.
Proof.
Algorithm 10 appears on pages 303-304 of [6]. Miller1 and also Algorithm 11 known as Miller2, if the
GRH is incorrect, might generate an incorrect answer. Furthermore, a constant c cited by Miller in [6] is
never explicitly calculated. Under GRH, Bach [2] calculated the constant c to be (upper bounded by) 2.
In Miller1 and Miller2 we thus use c = 2 from Bach [2].
Algorithm 10: Miller1(n): primality test algorithm
Input: n > 4 is odd; n−1 = 2kl, l odd
Output: n is Composite or Prime
1 n−1 = 2kl ; f(n) = c(lnn)2 ;
/* Bach [2] calculated c = 2 */
2 if PerfectPower(n) then
/* Line 1 */
3
return Composite ;
/* n is a perfect power n = ps, s ≥2 */
4 end
5 for a = 2,3,...,2(lnn)2 do
6
if (a|n)||(gcd(a,n) > 1) then
/* Line 2 (i) */
7
return Composite ;
/* Just a|n in [6] */
8

Algorithm 13 performs t times the tasks of Algorithm 12 which is a simpler probabilistic algorithm.
Algorithm 12 checks for a given a condition C1(a,n) and for all values m condition C2(a,n,m). The range
of values m depends on n since n−1 = 2k ·l, where k ≥1 and l odd determines the range of m as follows:
0 ≤m < k. Algorithm 13 repeats Algorithm 12 a number of times equal to t by drawing uniformly at
random an a from the range {2,3,...,n −2}. Algorithm 12 is weak algorithm and for this we call it
a compositeness test algorithm. The latter Algorithm 13 is a more reliable one and is referred to as a
primality test algorithm.
Theorem 4.9
Algorithm 13 is a probabilistic algorithm for testing whether n is composite or not.

In line 2 of Algorithm 12, or line 4 of Algorithm 13, an a is picked uniformly at random such that
2 ≤a < n−1.
Step 1. The condition n ∤a of Proposition 4.4 translates into gcd(a,n) > 1 since a < n, and this maps to
lines 3-5 of Algorithm 12, or lines 5-7 of Algorithm 13. If n is composite the algorithm terminates with
the correct answer Composite and a becomes a witness of the compositeness of n.
We are left with a sequence of steps to determine other wise the compositeness of n for an a such that
gcd(a,n) = 1.

report n to be composite and a to be the witness of its compositeness. This is a consequence of Eq.(4.31)
of Lemma 4.8.
Step 3. Otherwise n is either prime or composite.
A single execution of Steps 1, 2, and 3 is Algorithm 12. Repeat Step 1,2, and 3 a total of t times and
this leads to Algorithm 13.
Algorithm 13 tests for the two conditions C1(a,n) and C2(a,n,m) a number of times. We denote the
number of times these conditions are tested as t. Every time these conditions are tested we pick a random
a from the interval/set {2,3,...,n −2} of cardinality n −1. If for the chosen a, we have P1(a,n) and
P2(a,n,m) for all applicable m, then n is composite and a is the Rabin-Miller witness of the compositeness
of n and denoted as MRw(a,n), or the set of witnesses for n is denoted as MRw(n). Otherwise a is
a non-witness denoted MRnw(a,n) and the corresponding set MRnw(n). To simplify things we used
C1(a,n) = P1(a,n) and C2(a,n,m) = P2(a,n,m).

is reached. Therefore, if the algorithm is unsuccessful in proving the compositeness of n, Algorithm 13
exits through line 29. Pseudoprime then means that n can be composite (no proof of the compositeness
of n was found) or it can ble prime (and this is the reason no proof of the compositeness of n was found).

Proposition 4.5
Probability of successful reporting
If n is an odd composite number greater than four then Algorithm 13 returns Composite with probability
at least 3/4. The probability that the output of Algorithm 13 is Composite given that n is a composite
number is at least 1−4−t.
Proof. It was proved by Rabin [8] and independently by Monier [7] that for a composite odd n, |MRw(a)|
is at least 3φ(n)/4 and |MRnw(a)| is thus at most φ(n)/4.
But the result is applicable to Miller1.
Algorithm 13 uses the conditions of Miller2. Monier [7] proved that the conditions used in Miller1 and
Miller2 are equivalent. So we can use for this algorithm the claims of Rabin [8] and also of Monier [7].
Later, we introduce RabinMiller which was analyzed by Rabin [8].
Lemma 4.9
Running time of algorithm 13
The running time of Algorithm 13 is O(t · lgn · M(n)), where M(n) is the cost of multiplying n-bit
integers.
Proof. Exponentiation involves O(lgn) multiplications. Depending on how we implement integer multi-
plication the overall time complexity is O(t ·lgn·M(n)), where M(n) is the computational cost (bit model)
of multiplying n-bit integers.

Proposition 4.6
Equivalence of condition in Miller1 and Miller 2
Let n be an odd number n > 4 and that n−1 = 2k ·l, k ≥1 and l is odd. The conditions of Eq.(4.40) and
Eq.(4.41) utilized by MillerRabin are equivalent to the conditions of Eq.(4.24) and Eq.(4.31) utilized

Proof.
For both set of conditions we prove the equivalence for n being a prime number. The case n is composite
can be proved by symmetry using De Morgan’s Law. Consider the sequence of the d values forming the
following row vector in that case.

4.7.4
The Rabin-Miller primality test
Rabin [8] converted Algorithm 10 into a probabilistic primality test algorithm.
Proposition 4.7
Rabin [8]
Algorithm 15 is a probabilistic algorithm for testing whether n is prime or composite.
Proof.
Let W(a,n) [8] indicate that a is a witness of the compositeness of n. Then denote by W(n) the set of
all W(a,n) witnesses. Later on we we will use the terminology MRw(a,n) and MRw(n). for W(a,n) and
W(n) respectively.
W(n) = {a : W(a,n)}.
It was proved in [8] that for n > 4 and n composite
|W(n)| ≥3(n−1)
4
In a theorem to follow, it will be shown that
|MRnw(n)| ≤φ(n)
4
≤n−1
4
,
that implies
|MRw(n)| ≥3(n−1)
4
.
The proof of correctness appears in [6] and the derivations above [8]. We present later a proof that is
based on [5], with the latter originating from the paper of [7].

Proposition 4.8
Probability of successful reporting
If n is an odd composite number greater than four then Algorithm 15 returns Composite with probability
at least 3/4. The probability that the output of Algorithm 15 is Composite given that n is a composite
number is at least 1−4−t.
Proof. It was proved by Rabin [8] and independently by Monier [7] that for a composite odd n, |MRw(a)|
is at least 3φ(n)/4 and |MRnw(a)| is thus at most φ(n)/4.
Lemma 4.10
Running time of algorithm 15
The running time of Algorithm 15 is O(t · lgn · M(n)), where M(n) is the cost of multiplying n-bit
integers.
Proof. Exponentiation involves O(lgn) multiplications. Depending on how we implement integer multi-
plication the overall time complexity is O(t ·lgn·M(n)), where M(n) is the computational cost (bit model)
of multiplying n-bit integers.

We note that n = 13, we have n−1 = 22 ·3, where k = 2 and l = 3. The first column is a. The second

C2(a,n,0) is the case i.e. al ̸≡−1 (mod n). A + indicates so, a −indicates the opposite i.e. al ≡−1
(mod n).
The fifth column shows a21·l mod n. The sixth column indicates with a + whether C2(a,n,1) is the case
i.e. a21·l ̸≡−1 (mod n); otherwise a −is shown. In the seventh column a + indicates that a is a witness,
and an −that a is a non-witness to the compositeness of n.
For a ∈MRw(n) we need only +’s for the corresponding row. For a ∈MRnw(n) we need at least one
−or equivalently an n in the MRw(n) column. There are no witnesses to the compositeness of 13; we
know that 13 is a prime number not a composite number! We observe the number of MRw(a,n) is 0.

(mod p).

a2j+1·l ≡1
(mod p),
which implies
a2b(n)−1·l ≡1
(mod p).
The latter concludes the proof of this proposition.

Definition 5.1
A function f on Z+ is multiplicative if
f(ij) = f(i)f(j)
∀i ∈Z+,∀j ∈Z+,where gcd(i, j) = 1.
Lemma 5.1
If f, as previously defined, is multiplicative and not zero then f(1) = 1.
Proof. This derives for i = j = 1 and f(1) = f(1)f(1), provided that f(1) ̸= 0. Otherwise, since f is not
zero then there exists a k such that f(k) ̸= 0. Then f(k·1) = f(k)f(1) and dividing by f(k) ̸= 0 we reach
the same conclusion. Note that if f(1) = 0 then f(k ·1) = f(k)f(1) = 0, and therefore f(k) = 0 for all
k.
